Your daily briefing on artificial intelligence, from the world's leading tech sources
2026-10-16
21 sources · 388 articles · 4,906,361 characters

In the Last 24 Hours

A landmark essay from Goodfire researcher Nick Cammarata has shaken the interpretability community by arguing that neural networks are already legible, with long-standing cases of polysemanticity and superposition dissolving under agentic analysis. The same news cycle delivered two major open-weight model releases: Meta shipped Muse Spark Open, an 816-billion-parameter model now topping US open-weight benchmarks, while Reflection unveiled Beam, a 501-billion-parameter model pitched as fully ownable and customizable. Apple's MLX framework also made waves by enabling researchers to run frontier-class language models locally, with Muse Spark Open generating over 36 tokens per second across a cluster of four Mac Studios linked by RDMA.

On the consumer hardware front, Apple's first foldable phone, the iPhone Duo, dominated headlines as the company took the Apple Store offline ahead of a 5 AM Eastern preorder rush, with supply constrained to just seven to ten million units through the end of 2026. The device features a 7.6-inch inner display, a 5.4-inch outer display, the A20 Pro chip, and a $1999 starting price, though it trades Face ID for a different biometric approach. Accessories guides are already circulating, recommending the USB-C Apple Pencil, 40W and 100W fast-charging adapters, MagSafe car mounts, protective cases, and HomeKit security cameras for the upgraded StandBy mode.

Away from the big-ticket launches, HackerNoon compiled 290 free blog posts from its Gaming Fellowship programme, spanning Skyrim and Fallout 4 mod guides, Pokémon ROM hacks, console sales rankings, and walkthroughs for titles like Hollow Knight and Monster Hunter World, all ranked by reader engagement on the Learn Repo platform. Meanwhile, a two-year scientific test examining whether liquid metal thermal paste corrodes copper and aluminium components over extended use periods has sparked fresh debate among PC enthusiasts who adopted the compound as a popular cooling upgrade. Together, these stories paint a picture of a tech landscape where open AI models are maturing rapidly, Apple is betting big on foldables, and the enthusiast community continues to stress-test both software and hardware at the margins.

AI 研究圈近二十四個鐘頭可謂地震級消息。Goodfire 研究員 Nick Cammarata 發表咗一篇震撼性論文，話神經網絡其實已經可解釋，好多所謂嘅多義性同疊加現象用 agentic 分析一睇就散晒。同一時間，Meta 推出咗 816 億參數嘅 Muse Spark Open，而 Reflection 亦公布咗 501 億參數嘅 Beam 開源模型，主打自己擁有、自己改、自己 scale。Apple 嘅 MLX 框架都唔得閒，用四部 Mac Studio 串埋做 cluster，Muse Spark Open 可以跑到每秒超過 36 個 token，令本地跑前沿大模型真係變得可行。

消費電子方面，Apple 首款摺機 iPhone Duo 成咗全城焦點。Apple Store 喺東岸時間朝早五點預售之前直接關站，因為全年產量得七到一千萬部，預計秒殺。呢部機內屏 7.6 吋、外屏 5.4 吋、A20 Pro 晶片、起價一千九百九十九美金，不過放棄咗 Face ID。配件指南已經铺天盖地，USB-C Apple Pencil、40W 同 100W 快充頭、MagSafe 車架、保護殼、HomeKit 鏡頭，全部講晒點樣配合新機用。

唔係所有新聞都係大產品。HackerNoon 將 Gaming Fellowship 嘅 290 篇免費教學文整理晒出嚟，由 Skyrim、Fallout 4 改機指南到 Pokémon ROM hack、Hollow Knight 同 Monster Hunter World 攻略都有，按讀者互動排名放喺 Learn Repo 平台。另外仲有一條兩年期嘅科學測試片，研究液態金屬散熱膏會唔會長期腐蝕銅同鋁零件，喺 PC 發燒友之間引起好大討論。由開源大模型到摺機預售再到散熱膏腐蝕，呢一日嘅科技圈真係由雲端跑到桌面、由實驗室跑到玩家枱面。

Designing more efficient quantum circuits with Claude

https://www.anthropic.com/research/designing-efficient-quantum-circuits📅 2026-10-17 02:00 UTC+8▲ 22 ptsHN discussion

Quantum computers will need carefully designed circuits to run useful algorithms on limited hardware. We gave Claude plain-language specifications for eight such circuits, drawn from chemistry, optimization, physics simulation, and number theory, along with an automated evaluator that checks and scores each design. Working largely on its own, Claude found designs that improve on the best published circuits, in some cases by an order of magnitude or more. Such improvements substantially reduce the quantum resources that useful applications require, so practical quantum computers could arrive sooner than previously anticipated.

The Duo features a 7.6-inch inner display, a 5.4-inch outer display, the A20 Pro chip, and starts at $1999, though it trades Face ID for Touch ID and carries a camera downgrade due to its thin unfolded profile. How Windows is being optimized for Nvidia’s RTX Spark laptops

https://www.theverge.com/tech/1011996/microsoft-windows-rtx-spark-platform-changes-notepad📅 2026-10-16 03:09 UTC+8

Microsoft has spent months reworking Windows for Nvidia's new RTX Spark laptops, which pack Arm-based CPUs, Blackwell GPUs, and up to 128GB of unified memory to run powerful local AI models and AAA games. The changes include a streamlined two-click setup for AI agents like Hermes and OpenClaw, heavily tuned Prism emulation for x64 apps, and a unified memory slider that lets users balance resources between the CPU and GPU. This matters because it signals a genuine turning point for Windows on Arm, moving beyond the emulation headaches that plagued Qualcomm's Snapdragon laptops and finally delivering a credible gaming and AI experience on a non-x64 chip.

Integrating Netflix’s Foundation Model into Personalization applications

https://netflixtechblog.com/integrating-netflixs-foundation-model-into-personalization-applications-cf176b5860eb📅 2026-10-15 04:02 UTC+8

Netflix engineers detailed how they integrated a large Foundation Model into their personalization systems using three distinct approaches: generating embeddings via an Embedding Store, embedding the model as a subgraph within downstream models, and fine-tuning the model directly for specific applications. Each method trades off between freshness, computational cost, and integration complexity, with embeddings offering the lowest barrier to entry and subgraph integration unlocking the deepest model capabilities for high-impact use cases. This work fills a notable gap in the industry, as most published research covers training large transformer models but offers little practical guidance on wiring them into existing production recommendation pipelines

Measuring the Impact of Personalized Recommendations

https://netflixtechblog.com/measuring-the-impact-of-personalized-recommendations-4c26be3a4d96📅 2026-10-15 03:57 UTC+8

Netflix researchers have developed a low-rank choice model that separates a member's underlying viewing preferences from the incremental effect of personalized recommendations, solving the long-standing problem of distinguishing whether someone watched a title because it was suggested or because they would have found it anyway. The model, published in the International Journal of Industrial Organization and presented at ACM EC '26, treats daily viewing as a dynamic choice process and is validated against small-scale randomized experiments that nudge title visibility, confirming it can accurately predict how viewing shifts when recommendations change. This work matters because turning off personalization for a clean experiment would degrade the member experience, so a credible modelling approach is the only practical

In Part 1 of this series , we shared how Netflix built the Ads Event pipeline and approached it as a system design problem: replacing fragmented pipelines with a centralized collection, enrichment, and a standard data contract. The result was Ads Event Publisher, the system that collects ad telemetry from client devices, enriches it with the context of the ad that was actually served, and publishes a single, unified stream of ad events to every downstream consumer: frequency capping, metrics, billing, measurement partners, and reporting. Any member viewing or account details that move through this pipeline end-to-end are encrypted and handled in accordance with Netflix’s strict privacy and security practices to safeguard member PII.

A Netflix engineer spent two months untangling critical Out-Of-Memory failures in the member-sessionizer pipeline, which consolidates tens of billions of hourly client events into viewing sessions, only to discover that the real fix was not speeding up one run but firing off five parallel experiments at once using branch-isolated deployments. The root cause turned out to be collect_list buffering bloated event bags in memory, worsened by UUID strings replacing 8-byte longs and Scala Option wrappers inflating object allocation, and slimming individual event weight proved far more effective than adding cluster memory. The indicator uses the BBI line as its middle band and the standard deviation of that line to determine band width, forming a channel around price movements. The upper and lower bands can serve as references for potential resistance and support, although prices may move beyond either band. The middle band serves as a reference for distinguishing bullish from bearish conditions.

Issue 362

https://www.deeplearning.ai/the-batch/issue-362📅 2026-07-17 22:00 UTC+8

As AI increasingly automates coding, it frees up developers to spend time on high-level software development tasks traditionally reserved for senior engineers, like deciding on technical architecture and participating in scoping product requirements. Demand for this type of work is growing, since it is an economic complement to coding, which is becoming cheaper. This is why I’m confident there will be rising demand for broad AI engineering skills. I see a similar pattern starting to emerge in other AI-influenced fields as well, and am cautiously optimistic — contrary to predictions of a “ jobpocalypse ” — that AI will generally increase demand for people with the right skills. Take marketing. AI is helpful for drafting and editing marketing copy, gathering market data, and performing very basic data analysis. (In my experience, analyses by even frontier models are frequently wrong. So do use AI to help with your data-science tasks, but don’t blindly trust its confidently stated conclusions!) This frees up marketers to spend more time on higher-level tasks. I’m seeing that rather than specializing in narrow marketing roles like social media marketer or copy editor, AI-native marketers are rising up to help coordinate broader marketing campaigns end-to-end, from conception to multi-threaded execution to analyzing lessons learned. Just as AI is turning many specialized developers (like frontend, backend, mobile, etc.) into full-stack developers, I am seeing early signs that it is turning many more marketers into full-stack marketers. Or take recruiting. Some companies have separate roles for sourcer (who finds candidates online), coordinator (who handles scheduling) and recruiter (who runs the hiring process). But sourcing is increasingly automated, and coordination also partially automated. As a consequence, I’m seeing more AI-native recruiters run the full-cycle themselves, doing all of the above. As AI enters more fields, I expect more people to start to play “full-stack” or “full-cycle” roles within their disciplines, meaning they will play broader roles that integrate traditionally separate roles. This lets individuals do more, and I believe it will ultimately lead to increased demand for skills as well as higher pay.

Read full article →

346 words

Issue 361

https://www.deeplearning.ai/the-batch/issue-361📅 2026-07-10 22:00 UTC+8

Dear friends, The key idea of agentic coding loops is to have an agent keep working until it satisfies a condition, such as achieving a product specification. Coming up with the spec, the evals, or test set is one of the hardest tasks for many AI systems, and a key place to inject human knowledge. Once you have a clear spec, the paths to fulfilling it (such as having a coding agent iterate) become clearer. I'd like to share useful practices for building product specs that drive coding loops when quickly building 0-to-1 applications. As I'll explain below, one organizing principle I use is: AI tokens are cheap; human tokens are gold. (Yes, I know humans don't literally tokenize, but I work so much with tokens that I now think of myself as providing tokens to my computers!) When building a large, complex system, it is worthwhile to spend time architecting it well. This involves deciding, for example, what database do you want to use? How do you break down the workload across different services? What third-party dependencies are you locking yourself into? What are the API boundaries between frontend and backend? Thinking through such questions in advance helps to avoid being stuck with a bad decision that is hard to change. But when building quick 0-to-1 prototypes or applications in domains I'm unfamiliar with, I often move faster and with less deliberation. I find it convenient to jump quickly into directing a coding agent to start building, so I can examine what it has built in order to make the next set of decisions. No need to spend an hour of human time mulling over a design spec when an AI agent will spend 20 minutes building a simple prototype; I'd rather spend 10 minutes writing an inferior spec, see what the agent has built, examine its assumptions, and then refine the spec and repeat the process. Instead of "spec drive development" becoming a new waterfall process where writing a spec is a gate to further progress, this allows me to more iteratively refine the spec. For example, the first version of many apps I’ve prototyped had confusing UI designs that I modified the spec to fix, and no amount of my time spent tweaking the initial spec would have led me to realize in advance that these changes would be needed. Coming up with those human tokens on my own would have been too expensive. It was easier (and cheaper) to see what went wrong in order to explain how to do it right. As a software project matures, coding agents will work longer (maybe hours) to build to more complex specs, But at this early stage, it is fine to throw away the entire codebase and restart from scratch, so nothing is hard to change. All that code was cheap, in human time and token costs. What we learned from testing it was more valuable. Further, having one implementation to react to (or sometimes I'll ask the agent for a few different designs) gives you an efficient way to get feedback (human tokens) to refine the spec to help drive later iterations. If you're building a 0-to-1 product, I encourage you to put the coding agent to work, maybe even earlier than might feel comfortable, see what it comes up with, and use that as a starting point. Then document your reactions and keep iterating.

Read full article →

72 words

The keys to the Internet change on October 11. Are you ready?

https://blog.cloudflare.com/root-ksk-2024-rollover/📅 2026-10-07 01:50 UTC+8

On October 11, 2026, the DNS root is scheduled to change its key-signing key (KSK) for only the second time ever. This key anchors DNSSEC’s chain of trust, which lets DNS resolvers authenticate answers using cryptographic signatures. The change is called a KSK rollover. Validating resolvers need to trust the new key before the switch, as otherwise healthy websites could become unreachable.

Read full article →

97 words

Claude discovers a novel enzyme system with CRISPR-like repeats

https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-discovers-novel-enzyme-system📅 2026-09-24 00:06 UTC+8

We’re introducing a new life sciences research group and laboratory at Anthropic. Our focus is on fundamental biology research using Claude: exploring datasets of DNA to identify uncharacterized protein families, generating hypotheses at scale, and testing them through experiments in the lab. This post introduces the team behind this work and shares early results in which Claude discovered a novel enzyme system with properties reminiscent of CRISPR, with only high-level direction from our scientists. Many discoveries that have revolutionized biology and medicine started with a scientist noticing something odd in the staggering diversity of molecular machines found in nature. Restriction enzymes , proteins that cut DNA at specific short sequences , were found in bacterial immune systems, where they destroy the DNA of invading viruses. Researchers realized they could use these enzymes to cut DNA at chosen places and splice genes from one organism into another, which launched the biotechnology industry. Taq polymerase, an enzyme that copies DNA at high temperatures, was identified in a bacterium in a Yellowstone hot spring. It became the basis for PCR, the DNA-copying method used in much of modern diagnostics. CRISPR was first noticed as an unusual repeat sequence in the DNA of certain bacteria, and is now the foundation of gene editing-based medicines. In the spring of 2026, we formed a research group to see whether general AI models can systematize and accelerate such discoveries . We believe that this acceleration will come from establishing a new way of doing biology research, in which agents collaborate with humans in every step of the process. Developing this new way of working required that we build our own lab and a single team working on everything from training Claude in biology to running experiments in the lab.

Read full article →

AWS Architecture Blog 20 articles

How Dynata scaled AI across the development lifecycle with AI-DLC and Kiro IDE

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/how-dynata-scaled-ai-across-the-development-lifecycle-with-ai-dlc-and-kiro-ide/📅 2026-10-15 23:50 UTC+8

<p>When Dynata set out to compress multi-sprint feature delivery into a single sprint, the answer wasn’t simply faster coding. It was reimagining the entire development lifecycle with AI. The team progressed in deliberate stages. They began with AI-assisted coding, then connected AI with their knowledge ecosystem across Confluence, Jira, Lucidchart, GitHub, and Figma. Finally, they adopted the AI-Driven Development Lifecycle (AI-DLC) methodology from AWS using <a href="https://kiro.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Kiro</a> IDE, an AI-native development environment. In doing so, Dynata transformed how its cross-functional teams collaborate.</p> <p>Delivering software across multiple teams is a collaborative process that produces great outcomes when it works well. Product, project management, engineering, design, quality assurance (QA), and deployment each brings essential perspective, and that coordination requires significant time. Multiple rounds of discussion and review before development begins is common. For complex features, it’s expected and healthy.</p> <p>In this post, we show how Dynata reduced the development phase of features that typically spanned 3–4 sprints to approximately one sprint’s worth of time. We also share how you can bring AI into the full delivery process, not only individual developer output, giving every team earlier visibility and tighter alignment from the start.</p> <h2 id="about-dynata">About Dynata</h2> <p>Dynata is the world’s largest first-party data company, delivering precise, reliable insights that help businesses to make informed decisions at scale. Their platform empowers organizations to activate the right audiences, and measure impact with confidence through industry-leading data accuracy, commitment to continuous improvement, and operational excellence.</p> <p>Across Dynata’s portfolio, multiple distributed engineering teams work in parallel. They build and evolve platform capabilities, integrate with partner ecosystems, and ship features on continuous delivery cycles. Coordinating product intent, design, engineering, and test engineering across teams at that scale while maintaining delivery velocity is the kind of challenge that rewards better process as much as better technology.</p> <h2 id="the-challenge">The challenge</h2> <p>Dynata’s engineering teams had established a solid foundation that included experienced developers, modern cloud infrastructure on AWS, clear product ownership, and a culture of continuous improvement. As the organization scaled across multiple product lines and distributed teams, the natural next ambition was to make the delivery process faster and more connected across every team involved, including product, engineering, test engineering, and project management.</p> <p>The areas with the most headroom are familiar across enterprise engineering organizations. Requirements context was spread across Jira, Confluence, Lucid, and design documents, and bringing it all together into a shared, actionable picture before development started took meaningful time. Cross-functional alignment happened largely in sequence, which meant that input from test engineering, design, or deployment planning sometimes came later in the cycle than was ideal. And when scope shifted mid-sprint, absorbing the change required effort proportional to how far into implementation the team had progressed.</p> <p>Dynata’s goal was clear. The team wanted to bring AI into the delivery process in a way that accelerated the whole cycle, not only individual developer output, and gave every team earlier visibility and tighter alignment from the start.</p> <p>What followed was a structured, step-by-step approach starting at the code level, expanding to the knowledge ecosystem, and ultimately reaching across the full development lifecycle.</p> <h2 id="the-journey-from-code-generation-to-full-lifecycle-ai">The journey: From code generation to full lifecycle AI</h2> <p>Dynata’s path to AI-driven development didn’t happen in a single leap. It unfolded in three deliberate steps, each one building on the confidence and lessons of the one before. The team started by bringing AI into everyday coding, then connected it to the tools and context that teams already relied on and finally adopted a methodology that brought AI into every phase of delivery. Here is how each step played out.</p> <h3 id="step-1-ai-assisted-coding">Step 1: AI-assisted coding</h3> <p>Dynata’s developers began their AI journey with <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/q/developer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Q Developer</a>, the AI-powered coding assistant from AWS, using it for code generation, test writing, and implementation acceleration across day-to-day development work.</p> <p>The productivity gains were immediate and visible. Developers moved faster on implementation, generated higher volumes of well-structured code, and spent less time on repetitive scaffolding. The experience built genuine AI fluency across the engineering team, establishing AI-assisted development as a standard part of the workflow rather than an occasional experiment.</p> <p>As the team’s ambitions grew beyond code-level assistance, Dynata adopted Kiro IDE, an AI-native development environment from AWS built around spec-driven development, automated steering files, and native support for the AI-DLC workflow. Kiro gave the team the ability to connect AI to the full delivery process, from requirements through to implementation. The move was a natural progression, building on the AI fluency the team had already developed with Amazon Q Developer.</p> <blockquote> <p><em>“The code generation results were great from the start. Developers were more productive, and the quality held up.”</em></p> </blockquote> <h3 id="step-2-connecting-ai-to-the-knowledge-ecosystem">Step 2: Connecting AI to the knowledge ecosystem</h3> <p>With a productive coding baseline in place, the team explored what AI could do when given access to broader project context. They integrated their AI tooling with Jira, Confluence, Lucid, and other knowledge sources, giving the AI visibility into tickets, specifications, architecture diagrams, and design documentation alongside the code base.</p> <p>The value expanded significantly. Tech leads and architects began using AI not only for implementation, but for architectural decision support. They used it to review design choices against existing system context, identify potential conflicts with other components, and validate alignment with established patterns at a per-developer level.</p> <blockquote> <p><em>“Once we connected AI with Jira, Confluence, and Lucid, we started using it not just for code but for architectural design at each developer level. The results were also great.”</em></p> </blockquote> <h3 id="step-3-discovering-ai-dlc">Step 3: Discovering AI-DLC</h3> <p>With AI delivering value at the coding and architectural design stages, the team set its sights on a broader goal. They wanted AI to help with the entire delivery process, from requirements through to production, not just implementation alone.</p> <p>The team discovered AI-DLC, the AI-Driven Development Lifecycle methodology from AWS, through AWS blog posts and published customer success stories. The methodology positions AI as a structured part of every phase of software delivery, spanning requirements capture and clarification, design, task planning, and implementation, with human review and approval at each stage. AI-DLC is powered by <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock</a>, which provides the foundation model layer behind the AI analysis, generation, and clarification in Kiro.</p> <blockquote> <p><em>“We were looking for solutions to include AI across the lifecycle. Then we saw the AWS AI-DLC posts and success stories and wanted to try it internally.”</em></p> </blockquote> <h2 id="adoption-a-staged-approach-that-built-confidence-at-every-step">Adoption: A staged approach that built confidence at every step</h2> <p>Dynata took a deliberate, staged adoption path. The team built expertise and refined the process before involving the broader organization. This turned out to be one of the most valuable decisions they made.</p> <h3 id="stage-1-internal-pilot-with-interested-developers">Stage 1: Internal pilot with interested developers</h3> <p>A small group of developers who were motivated to explore tried AI-DLC independently using Kiro IDE. Kiro implements the AI-DLC workflow natively through Specs (structured requirements-through-tasks pipelines), steering files (team coding standards injected into every AI session), and hooks (automated quality checks at defined workflow points).</p> <p>What the pilot showed was qualitatively different from standard AI code generation. Rather than only producing code, AI-DLC was helping the process by guiding structured iterations, surfacing gaps and ambiguities proactively, and building confidence in the design before a line of implementation code was written.</p> <blockquote> <p><em>“Instead of just generating code, it was helping the process – reviewing, identifying gaps, iterating- giving confidence that it would work at a large scale.”</em></p> </blockquote> <p>The pilot also surfaced what needed to be in place before scaling. Steering files needed tuning to produce code at the team’s production standards. The person leading a team session needed solid familiarity with the AI-DLC workflow. And participants benefited from a shared understanding of what the process involved before sitting down together.</p> <p>It’s worth noting that AI-DLC is IDE-agnostic. Although Kiro provides native support for the full workflow, teams at Dynata also ran AI-DLC sessions using Cursor with Claude and found the methodology worked effectively across environments. What matters is the structured process, and the tooling supports it.</p> <h3 id="stage-2-preparation-before-team-sessions">Stage 2: Preparation before team sessions</h3> <p>Before running a full cross-functional workshop, three things needed to be in place:</p> <p><strong>Session leader readiness.</strong> The person guiding the session, typically a tech lead or senior developer, needed working familiarity with the AI-DLC methodology and the IDE being used. Not expert-level mastery, but enough to keep the team moving through each phase, handle questions, and recognize when to spend more time on a step.</p> <p><strong>Team orientation.</strong> All participants including product, project management, tech leads, and developers received a high-level briefing on what AI-DLC is, how the session would be structured, what to expect from each phase, and what their role was. This upfront alignment made sessions significantly more productive.</p> <p><strong>Input preparation.</strong> Steering files describing the team’s coding standards, naming conventions, and architectural patterns were prepared in advance. Jira epics, Confluence specifications, and design documents were gathered and accessible before the session began. The quality of AI output is directly connected to the completeness of the context it is given.</p> <h3 id="stage-3-the-three-day-ai-dlc-feature-workshop">Stage 3: The three-day AI-DLC feature workshop</h3> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/12/ARCHBLOG-1747-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/12/ARCHBLOG-1747-1.png" alt="Comparison of traditional sequential delivery and AI-DLC delivery, with all teams aligned from inception through code generation" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1: Traditional feature delivery compared to delivery with AI-DLC at Dynata</p> </div> <p>With preparation complete and pilot learnings incorporated, the team selected a feature and ran a structured three-day workshop. This is where the methodology demonstrated its full value.</p> <p>The workshop brought together product, the project owner, tech leads, and developers working through the AI-DLC workflow in Kiro IDE together.</p> <h4 id="day-1-requirements-and-alignment">Day 1: Requirements and alignment</h4> <p>The AI-DLC workflow guided the team step by step through the Inception phase:</p> <ul> <li>The AI analyzed all available context, including Jira epics, Confluence pages, design documents, and the existing code base, and generated a consolidated, structured requirements view.</li> <li>The AI surfaced clarifying questions, such as gaps between sources, edge cases without defined behavior, and technology decisions that hadn’t been made explicit. The team worked through each question together, with answers captured directly in the IDE.</li> <li>All stakeholders reviewed and validated the output together. This was often the first time the full team had seen a unified picture of what was being built, with role-specific documents giving each participant the level of detail relevant to their work.</li> </ul> <p>By the end of the day, requirements were agreed upon, documented, and committed to the repository.</p> <p>The difference came down to timing. Instead of blockers and ambiguities surfacing during implementation, when resolving them is most expensive, they surfaced during planning, when the cost of a correction is a conversation. A process that typically took days or weeks of iterative meetings across teams was completed in a single structured day.</p> <h4 id="day-2-design-and-task-planning">Day 2: Design and task planning</h4> <p>With agreed requirements in hand, the team moved into design:</p> <ul> <li>The AI generated component boundaries, service interfaces, data models, API contracts, and dependency maps based on the approved requirements.</li> <li>Tech leads and senior developers reviewed the design artifacts, applied their judgment, and validated that the design reflected real system constraints.</li> <li>When design questions connected back to requirements, the team updated the requirements documents and the AI kept the design documents consistent. Changes propagated through the artifact chain automatically.</li> <li>Product and project management remained in the room throughout. For many, this was the first direct view into how engineering translates requirements into architecture. When design questions required a business decision, the right people were immediately available.</li> <li>After design was approved, the AI generated a sequenced task list, each task mapped to design artifacts and requirements, with implementation detail for each.</li> <li>The team reviewed and iterated on the task list, then distributed ownership so that each developer took responsibility for specific tasks.</li> </ul> <p>The team also made one practical observation. Thoughtful task distribution upfront, accounting for component dependencies and parallel workstreams, reduces merge conflicts during development. This was something the team refined over the first one or two feature cycles and improved steadily.</p> <p><strong>Outcome.</strong> The team approved design and tasks by the end of the day, with every team member clear on what was being built and who owned what.</p> <h4 id="day-3-code-generation-and-working-software">Day 3: Code generation and working software</h4> <p>With tasks distributed and owners assigned, developers worked through code generation:</p> <ul> <li>The AI generated code grounded in the approved design documents, requirements, and user stories with steering files ensuring output conformed to the team’s coding standards from the start.</li> <li>Before generating code for each unit, the AI produced a detailed code generation plan for the developer to review and approve, keeping developers in control of what was being built and why.</li> <li>All documents and artifacts from Days 1 and 2 were committed to the repository, giving every developer a shared, versioned baseline to work from.</li> </ul> <p>Progress was demoed to the team at regular intervals for directional feedback, keeping everyone aligned as the feature took shape.</p> <blockquote> <p><em>“After a few features built using AI-DLC, the quality of the end-to-end results and the code quality showed great improvements.”</em></p> </blockquote> <p>Steering files improved with each feature. As the team accumulated more experience describing their standards and conventions, code generation output became more consistent and required less adjustment, a compounding return on the investment in getting the inputs right.</p> <p><strong>Outcome.</strong> By the end of Day 3, the team had a working, demonstrable state of the feature, with the development phase completed and the full scope visible and validated. They also had a clear picture of what remained, including testing, edge case hardening, and release preparation. This isn’t the finish line. It’s a strong, confident starting point for the steps that follow. What it reduces is the uncertainty that normally exists at this stage, because requirements gaps, design misalignments, and implementation surprises are already behind the team, not ahead of them.</p> <h2 id="beyond-full-workshops-using-ai-dlc-selectively">Beyond full workshops: Using AI-DLC selectively</h2> <p>After running full three-day workshops for several features, the team discovered something important about the methodology. AI-DLC is flexible, and not every situation calls for the full workflow. Teams began using individual phases of AI-DLC independently, based on what they were trying to achieve:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Requirements discussions between product and tech teams.</strong> Using only the Inception phase to structure a requirements conversation, surface ambiguities, and produce a shared document, without necessarily proceeding to design or code generation in the same session.</li> <li><strong>Design-only sessions.</strong> Working through the design phase for a well-understood feature to align on architecture before developers began implementation.</li> <li><strong>Code generation from existing documentation.</strong> Teams with well-documented requirements using only the construction phase to accelerate implementation.</li> </ul> <p>The methodology works as a complete end-to-end workflow, and it works as a targeted tool for specific parts of the process. Teams adopted it wherever it added the most value for their situation, which is the right way to use it.</p> <h2 id="observations-and-what-accelerated-progress">Observations and what accelerated progress</h2> <p>Throughout the journey, from AI-assisted coding through to full AI-DLC workshops, the Dynata team identified a set of practical observations that shaped how they adopted the methodology and what made the biggest difference. These aren’t theoretical recommendations. They come directly from what the team experienced across multiple features, teams, and sessions.</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Observation</strong></td> <td><strong>What the team found</strong></td> <td><strong>What helped</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Steering file quality</td> <td>Early code generation output needed adjustment to fully match team standards</td> <td>Iterative refinement of steering files across features. Quality improved steadily and compounded</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Session leader readiness</td> <td>Sessions moved best when the leader had hands-on experience with the workflow</td> <td>Pilot developers led early team sessions. Pre-session preparation for leaders paid dividends</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Input context completeness</td> <td>Richer input context produced better AI output</td> <td>Pre-session effort to gather and organize source materials consistently improved session quality</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Task distribution planning</td> <td>Parallel development on related components required thoughtful dependency planning</td> <td>Spending time on task distribution during the workshop, not after, reduced rework in subsequent steps</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Team orientation</td> <td>Participants who understood the process upfront contributed more effectively</td> <td>A brief pre-session briefing for all participants improved engagement and session pace</td> </tr> <tr> <td>IDE flexibility</td> <td>Teams used both Kiro and Cursor with Claude successfully</td> <td>AI-DLC is IDE-agnostic. What matters is the structured process, not the specific tooling</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <h2 id="results">Results</h2> <p>Across features where Dynata applied the full AI-DLC workflow, the results were consistent:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Approximately 70 percent reduction in development phase time:</strong> By Dynata’s own estimate, based on their standard sprint planning, features that would typically span about four sprints were completed in roughly 1 sprint of developer time, with working software demonstrable at the end of the structured workshop.</li> <li><strong>Fewer post-production iterations and bugs.</strong> By surfacing and resolving requirements gaps and design ambiguities during the planning phase, before code was written, the team saw noticeably lower post-launch rework compared to features built through traditional delivery cycles.</li> <li><strong>Requirements alignment in a single session.</strong> A process that previously took days to weeks of iterative meetings across teams was consistently completed on Day 1 of the structured workshop.</li> <li><strong>Compounding quality improvements.</strong> With each successive feature built using AI-DLC, code generation quality improved as steering files were refined, reducing the adjustment needed after generation and raising the baseline for all subsequent features.</li> </ul> <p>The following table summarizes the delivery impact.</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Metric</strong></td> <td><strong>Traditional delivery</strong></td> <td><strong>With AI-DLC</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Development phase time</td> <td>Baseline</td> <td>~70% reduction</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Development phase for a full-stack feature</td> <td>3–4 sprints</td> <td>Approximately 1 sprint</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Time to agreed requirements</td> <td>Days to weeks of iterative review</td> <td>Day 1 of a structured session</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Requirement gaps and blockers</td> <td>Surfaced during implementation</td> <td>Surfaced during planning, before design or code begins</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Scope changes</td> <td>Absorbed mid-implementation, cost grows with progress</td> <td>Resolved at design level before code is written</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Cross-team alignment</td> <td>Sequential, with each team engaged at their stage</td> <td>Parallel, with all teams present and aligned from Day 1</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Post-production bugs and rework</td> <td>Higher gaps discovered post-implementation</td> <td>Reduced ambiguities resolved upfront during planning</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Code quality consistency</td> <td>Dependent on individual developer conventions</td> <td>Consistent, enforced through steering files from the first line</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p>The result is not only faster delivery. It is a different way of working across the full lifecycle. Alignment happens at inception and continues throughout development. Every team has visibility into what is being built, contributes feedback without waiting for a handoff, and sees progress in real time.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>By adopting AI-DLC with Kiro IDE, Dynata reduced the development phase of full-stack features from 3–4 sprints to approximately one sprint. Dynata achieved this not by adding engineers or cutting scope, but by aligning every team on a shared plan before the first line of code is written. Requirement gaps surface during planning. Teams absorb scope changes at the design level. The repository preserves institutional knowledge as structured, traceable artifacts.</p> <p>Dynata is expanding AI-DLC adoption across additional product lines and engineering teams, applying the methodology to more complex integration and infrastructure work. The team continues to refine steering files across projects and is exploring Kiro IDE hooks to automate quality enforcement at defined workflow points.</p> <p>Building on the flexibility that let teams apply individual phases independently, Dynata is now planning <a href="https://github.com/awslabs/aidlc-workflows/tree/main#quick-start" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AI-DLC V2.0</a>. This version adds support for targeted phases, so teams can invoke a specific part of the workflow, such as Inception, design, or construction, on its own. They no longer need to step through the full end-to-end cycle. This gives teams finer-grained control to apply AI-DLC exactly where it delivers the most value for a given piece of work.</p> <blockquote> <p><em>“Once the requirements, design, and tasks are all agreed upon with the full team, delivery becomes much faster, and the whole team is part of that journey, not just engineering.”</em></p> </blockquote> <h2 id="get-started">Get started</h2> <ul> <li>Read <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/devops/ai-driven-development-life-cycle/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Introducing AI-DLC: AI-Driven Development Life Cycle: Reimagining Software Engineering</a>.</li> <li>Explore the <a href="https://github.com/awslabs/aidlc-workflows" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AI-DLC workflows</a> on GitHub.</li> <li>Try <a href="https://kiro.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Kiro</a> IDE and review the <a href="https://kiro.dev/docs/specs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Specs</a> and <a href="https://kiro.dev/docs/steering/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">steering files</a> documentation.</li> <li>Learn about <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock</a>, the foundation model service powering the AI in Kiro, and read the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/what-is-bedrock.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Bedrock documentation</a>.</li> </ul> <p>&nbsp;</p> <p style="clear: both"></p> <hr style="width: 100%"> <h2>About the authors</h2>

Multi-region disaster recovery for Amazon API Gateway WebSocket APIs

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/multi-region-disaster-recovery-for-amazon-api-gateway-websocket-apis/📅 2026-10-14 22:31 UTC+8

<p>WebSocket connections power some of the most interactive experiences on the web: financial trading applications streaming live market data, collaborative editing tools where multiple users work on the same document, gaming leaderboards updating in real time, and IoT dashboards monitoring thousands of devices. These applications depend on persistent, bidirectional communication between clients and servers.</p> <p>Real-time applications using WebSocket connections face a unique challenge during Regional outages. WebSocket connections are persistent and bound to a specific AWS Region. A Regional failure severs every active connection. For applications that also maintain shared state across those connections, such as a live auction or collaborative editor, users lose both their connection and their session context. This post presents a design pattern for multi-Region disaster recovery with <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/api-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon API Gateway</a> WebSocket APIs that reconnects clients and restores state automatically during failover.</p> <p>In this post, you learn how to build this pattern using <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/route53/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Route 53</a> for DNS-based failover, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB</a> Global Tables for cross-Region state replication, a canary health probe for traffic-independent failure detection, and client-side reconnection logic to recover from Regional failures while preserving application state.</p> <h2 id="solution-overview">Solution overview</h2> <p>This pattern creates an active-passive multi-Region architecture where the primary Region serves WebSocket connections and automatically fails over to a secondary Region during a disaster. The core idea is separating application state from the connection itself, so the client can restore its session after reconnection.</p> <p>The architecture deploys identical WebSocket API stacks in two Regions, a primary and a secondary. Both Regions share application state through DynamoDB Global Tables, so the secondary Region is consistently ready to serve traffic. A single custom domain name fronts both Regions. <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/route53/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Route 53</a> <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/Route53/latest/DeveloperGuide/routing-policy-failover.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">failover routing policy</a> directs traffic to the primary Region under normal conditions. When the primary becomes unhealthy, Route 53 automatically shifts DNS resolution to the secondary Region. Clients reconnect to the same domain and resume operations without changing their configuration.</p> <p>Figure 1 shows the architecture and the normal operation and failover flow.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/12/ARCHBLOG-1349-1-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/12/ARCHBLOG-1349-1-2.png" alt="Architecture diagram showing a client connecting through a Route 53 failover policy to the primary Region us-east-1, which runs API Gateway WebSocket, Lambda, and DynamoDB, with an Amazon EventBridge rule triggering a canary health check and a CloudWatch alarm that drives the Route 53 health check; DynamoDB Global Tables replicate to the secondary Region us-west-2 for failover" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1: Multi-region WebSocket API failover architecture</p> </div> <ol type="1"> <li>A user opens the application, which establishes a WebSocket connection to the application’s domain name.</li> <li>Amazon Route 53 resolves the domain using a failover routing policy. Under normal conditions, it returns the primary Region’s endpoint.</li> <li>The WebSocket connection reaches API Gateway in the primary Region, which invokes an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lambda</a> function for each connection event and message.</li> <li>Lambda reads and writes application state to DynamoDB. Connected clients receive updates through their open WebSocket connections.</li> <li>DynamoDB Global Tables replicates the application state to the secondary Region asynchronously, keeping it ready to serve traffic.</li> <li>Independently, an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EventBridge</a> rule triggers a canary Lambda function every minute.</li> <li>The canary opens a WebSocket connection to the API Gateway endpoint, sends a heartbeat, and publishes the result as a custom <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon CloudWatch</a> metric.</li> <li>A CloudWatch alarm evaluates the metric. If the canary reports consecutive failures or stops publishing, the alarm fires and the associated Route 53 health check marks the primary as unhealthy.</li> <li>Route 53 detects the unhealthy primary and begins resolving the domain to the secondary Region instead.</li> <li>Clients reconnect through the same domain, now reaching the secondary Region, and recover their application state from the local DynamoDB replica.</li> </ol> <h2 id="prerequisites">Prerequisites</h2> <p>To follow along with this post, you need:</p> <ul> <li>An <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/free/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS account</a> with permissions to create Amazon API Gateway, AWS Lambda, Amazon DynamoDB, Amazon CloudWatch, and Route 53 resources.</li> <li>A domain name with a hosted zone in <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/Route53/latest/DeveloperGuide/CreatingHostedZone.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Route 53</a>.</li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/certificate-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Certificate Manager</a> (ACM) certificates for your domain in both the primary and secondary Regions.</li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/getting-started-install.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Command Line Interface</a> (AWS CLI) v2 installed and configured.</li> <li><a href="https://nodejs.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Node.js</a> 18.x or later (for the client application).</li> </ul> <h2 id="deploy-the-solution">Deploy the solution</h2> <p>The complete source code and deployment instructions are available on the <a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-apigw-websocket-multi-region-disaster-recovery" target="_blank" rel="noopener">GitHub repo</a>. The repository includes AWS CloudFormation templates for the WebSocket APIs, canary health probe, and a React client application with the reconnection logic described in this post.</p> <h2 id="the-demo-application">The demo application</h2> <p>The sample application is a real-time auction where multiple users bid on an item simultaneously. Each bid updates a shared counter through WebSocket messages, and connected clients see the current bid in real time. The application displays which AWS Region is serving responses, making failover visible during testing. Although minimal, this pattern represents real-time workloads where multiple clients share mutable state over WebSocket connections: chat applications, collaborative editors, live dashboards, or multiplayer games.</p> <h2 id="managing-connection-state-across-regions">Managing connection state across Regions</h2> <p>WebSocket connections are persistent, but the application state built on top of them is what matters during failover. As users interact with the application, the server builds up session data tied to their identity. When a failover occurs and the client reconnects to a different Region, that session data needs to be available for the experience to continue uninterrupted. This solution stores state in DynamoDB Global Tables, which replicates it to the secondary Region asynchronously, typically within a second. Because this replication is asynchronous, writes that occur immediately before a failure may not reach the secondary Region. For most applications, this window is small enough to be acceptable.</p> <p>After failover, the client reconnects and restores its state. In this demo, the client sends a <code>getState</code> message after reconnection to retrieve the latest application state from the secondary Region’s DynamoDB replica.</p> <p>Production applications add two considerations on reconnection. First, clients authenticate again: WebSocket APIs authenticate on the <code>$connect</code> route, so after failover the secondary Region re-authenticates the client through a <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/apigateway/latest/developerguide/apigateway-websocket-api-lambda-auth.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Lambda authorizer</a>. Deploy that authorizer in both Regions so reconnecting clients can authenticate against either one. Second, to restore state, identify the user from the validated token, such as the <code>sub</code> claim. Key the stored state by the user ID rather than the connection ID, which changes on every reconnect. Applications model session state in different ways, so adapt this to your design.</p> <p>If your application uses a relational database, you need to set up similar cross-region state replication. For instance, <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonRDS/latest/AuroraUserGuide/aurora-global-database.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Aurora Global Database</a> provides sub-second replication latency across Regions. Note that Aurora requires promoting a read replica to primary during failover, which typically adds 1–2 minutes to the recovery process. This promotion can also be automated based on a CloudWatch alarm action.</p> <h2 id="heartbeat">Heartbeat</h2> <p>Both the client and the canary use application-level heartbeat to verify their WebSocket connections are alive. At a regular interval, they send a lightweight message and expect an acknowledgment. A missing or invalid response signals a broken connection. The client uses this to trigger reconnection. The canary uses it to drive the Route 53 health check. This mechanism is described in <a href="https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc6455" target="_blank" rel="noopener">RFC 6455</a> and is the standard approach for WebSocket connection health monitoring.</p> <p>The client code snippets in this post use <a href="https://www.npmjs.com/package/react-use-websocket" target="_blank" rel="noopener">react-use-websocket</a>, a React library that provides built-in support for reconnection and heartbeat. You can implement the same patterns in other WebSocket client libraries or languages.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-javascript">heartbeat: { message: JSON.stringify({ action: 'heartbeat' }), returnMessage: 'pong', timeout: 35000, // Close connection if no message received within 35s interval: 30000, // Send heartbeat every 30s }</code></pre> </div> <h2 id="detecting-regional-failures-automatically">Detecting regional failures automatically</h2> <p>When the primary Region becomes unavailable, Route 53 needs to detect the failure and redirect traffic to the secondary. To do this, the solution creates a health check that actively monitors the WebSocket endpoint. A Lambda function called the canary, deployed in the primary Region, powers this health check. It probes the endpoint every minute by opening a WebSocket connection, sending a heartbeat message, and confirming it receives a valid response. The canary publishes the result as a custom CloudWatch metric (<code>App/WebSocketHealth</code>, dimension <code>ApiId</code>): 1 for healthy, 0 for unhealthy.</p> <p>A CloudWatch alarm monitors this metric and treats both failures and missing data as unhealthy (<code>TreatMissingData: breaching</code>). After two consecutive unhealthy datapoints, the alarm fires and the associated Route 53 health check (configured with <code>InsufficientDataHealthStatus: Unhealthy</code>) marks the primary as down. Route 53 shifts DNS to the secondary Region.</p> <p>Failback works through the same mechanism. When the primary recovers, the canary starts succeeding again, the alarm clears, and Route 53 shifts traffic back. For production workloads, consider asymmetric thresholds: use a low threshold (two datapoints) for fast failover and a higher threshold (over five) for slower failback, which helps prevent flapping if the primary is unstable during recovery. A DNS time-to-live (TTL) of 60–120 seconds provides a good balance between fast propagation and lookup efficiency.</p> <h2 id="client-reconnection-strategy">Client reconnection strategy</h2> <p>When a connection drops, the client needs to reconnect without overwhelming the recovering service. The heartbeat described earlier handles detection in most cases. If no messages arrive within the timeout window, the library closes the connection. However, the backend can be degraded rather than fully unreachable. For example, API Gateway returns error responses when the Lambda function is throttled, so the connection remains technically open while the application is non-functional. To handle this, the client tracks consecutive failed heartbeats: responses that aren’t the expected <code>pong</code> message. After 2 consecutive failures, the client force-closes the connection and triggers reconnection. With a 30-second heartbeat interval, this detects a degraded backend within 60 seconds.</p> <p>After the client identifies a broken connection, it begins reconnecting using exponential backoff with full jitter, a pattern described in the AWS Architecture Blog post <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/exponential-backoff-and-jitter/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Exponential Backoff And Jitter</a>. The reconnection interval doubles with each attempt, and full jitter randomizes the actual delay within that window. This spreads reconnection attempts over time, helping prevent a thundering herd where many clients retry simultaneously and overwhelm the secondary Region the moment DNS shifts.</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-javascript">reconnectInterval: (attempt) =&gt; { // Exponential backoff with full jitter const expDelay = Math.min(2000 * Math.pow(2, attempt), 20000); return Math.random() * expDelay; }</code></pre> </div> <p>After each successful reconnection, the client requests the current application state from the server. Because the client reconnects to the same custom domain, Route 53 resolves it to whichever Region is currently healthy. The client doesn’t need to know which Region it’s connecting to. The server includes the Region in its response, and the application can update its UI or logging accordingly.</p> <h2 id="failover-in-action">Failover in action</h2> <p>To see the full failover sequence, start the client application and connect to your WebSocket endpoint:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">cd client cp .env.example .env.local # Edit .env.local with your custom domain URL npm install npm run dev</code></pre> </div> <p>Open the application in your browser and note the serving Region displayed in the UI.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/12/ARCHBLOG-1349-2-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/12/ARCHBLOG-1349-2-2.png" alt="Auction app showing Connected status and serving Region US-EAST-1 with a current bid of $9" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 2: Client connected to the primary Region (us-east-1)</p> </div> <p>Next, simulate a failure by setting the primary Lambda function’s reserved concurrency to zero, which prevents it from processing requests:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">aws lambda put-function-concurrency \ --function-name counter-handler-us-east-1 \ --reserved-concurrent-executions 0 \ --region us-east-1</code></pre> </div> <p>Within about two minutes, the canary detects the failure after two consecutive failed probes at one-minute intervals. Route 53 then marks the primary as unhealthy, and DNS begins resolving to the secondary Region.</p> <p>The client detects the dropped connection and begins retrying with exponential backoff and jitter.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/12/ARCHBLOG-1349-3-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/12/ARCHBLOG-1349-3-2.png" alt="Auction app showing Disconnected status and reconnecting after a connection failure, with connection errors in the activity log" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 3: Client detecting failure and reconnecting with exponential backoff</p> </div> <p>When DNS propagation completes, the next reconnection attempt reaches the secondary Region. The client requests the current state, recovers it from DynamoDB Global Tables, and the UI updates to show the new serving Region.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/12/ARCHBLOG-1349-4-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/10/12/ARCHBLOG-1349-4-1.png" alt="Auction app reconnected to serving Region US-WEST-2 after DNS failover, with the current bid of $9 preserved" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 4: Client after failover to the secondary Region (us-west-2) with state preserved</p> </div> <p>When the primary Region recovers, the canary confirms that it’s healthy again. After the alarm clears, Route 53 shifts DNS back and clients reconnect to the primary through the same automatic process. No manual intervention is needed for failback.</p> <h2 id="clean-up">Clean up</h2> <p>To avoid ongoing charges, delete the resources created during this walkthrough:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Delete the CloudFormation stacks: <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">aws cloudformation delete-stack --stack-name websocket-health-check --region us-east-1 aws cloudformation delete-stack --stack-name websocket-counter-secondary --region us-west-2 aws cloudformation delete-stack --stack-name websocket-counter-primary --region us-east-1</code></pre> </div></li> <li>Delete the Route 53 failover records and API Gateway custom domain names manually from the console.</li> </ol> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>In this post, you learned how to build a multi-region disaster recovery pattern for API Gateway WebSocket APIs. By separating application state from the connection itself, you can restore sessions automatically after a regional failover. This pattern applies to real-time applications where WebSocket continuity matters. Adapt the health check thresholds, reconnection timing, and state recovery approach to fit your workload.</p> <p>To learn more about the services used in this solution, refer to the following resources:</p> <ul> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/apigateway/latest/developerguide/apigateway-websocket-api.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon API Gateway WebSocket APIs</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/GlobalTables.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon DynamoDB Global Tables</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/apigateway/latest/developerguide/dns-failover.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Configure custom health checks for DNS failover</a></li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/exponential-backoff-and-jitter/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Exponential Backoff And Jitter</a></li> </ul> <p>&nbsp;</p> <p style="clear: both"></p> <hr style="width: 100%"> <h2>About the authors</h2>
