Deploy Oracle Database step by step on Amazon EVS with FSx for ONTAP

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/deploy-oracle-database-step-by-step-on-amazon-evs-with-fsx-for-ontap/📅 2026-10-03 01:57 UTC+8

<hr> <p>This post provides step-by-step procedures to deploy Oracle Database on <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/evs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Elastic VMware Service (Amazon EVS)</a> with <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fsx/netapp-ontap/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP</a> as NFS datastore storage. You will provision storage volumes, mount NFS datastores, install Oracle, and configure SnapMirror replication for cross-region disaster recovery.</p> <p>Enterprises running Oracle databases on VMware want a path to AWS that preserves their existing operational workflows with no rearchitecting and retraining. In our related post, <a href="https://repost.aws/articles/ARLXLyCCi1R_692Ww9cIiXyA" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Architect highly available Oracle Database on Amazon EVS and FSx for ONTAP</a>, we explained how to design that environment: selecting <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) bare metal instances</a>, sizing VMs, splitting storage between vSAN and FSx for NetApp ONTAP, and planning SnapMirror replication for cross-region DR.</p> <p>This post picks up where the architecture left off. We provide step-by-step procedures to deploy the entire stack — from provisioning your first Oracle VM and creating FSx for ONTAP volumes, through mounting NFS datastores, installing Oracle 19c, and configuring SnapMirror and SnapCenter. We also cover four migration paths for moving existing on-premises Oracle workloads to EVS and day-2 operations including snapshot backup, point-in-time recovery, and database cloning.</p> <p>For architecture decisions, instance type selection, storage design rationale, and high availability planning, see our related post: <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/architect-highly-available-oracle-database-on-amazon-evs-and-fsx-for-ontap/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Architect highly available Oracle Database on Amazon EVS and FSx for ONTAP</a>.</p> <hr> <h2 id="step-by-step-deployment-procedures">Step-by-step deployment procedures</h2> <p>This section walks through the end-to-end deployment workflow: provisioning the Oracle VM, creating FSx for ONTAP storage volumes, mounting NFS datastores in vSphere, installing Oracle 19c, and configuring SnapMirror for cross-region DR. Complete the prerequisites first, then follow Steps 1 through 8 in order.</p> <h3 id="prerequisites">Prerequisites</h3> <ul> <li>Amazon EVS environment deployed (VCF 9.1, ESXi 9.1. With VCF 9.x, Amazon EVS provisions the bare metal infrastructure and VLANs and you deploy VCF using Broadcom’s VCF Installer).</li> <li>Minimum 4 hosts per cluster. Dedicated DB cluster recommended for Oracle.</li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Virtual private cloud (VPC)</a> with <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/vpc/latest/userguide/route-server.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Route Server</a> endpoints, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/transit-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Transit Gateway</a>, and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/directconnect/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Direct Connect</a> configured.</li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)</a> permissions for FSx for ONTAP provisioning.</li> <li>VCF license entitlement (BYOL from Broadcom).</li> </ul> <hr> <h3 id="step-1-deploy-oracle-vm-on-evs">Step 1: Deploy Oracle VM on EVS</h3> <ol type="1"> <li>Log in to the vSphere Client connected to your EVS vCenter.</li> <li>Create a new Virtual Machine in the Production DB Cluster: <ul> <li><strong>Guest OS:</strong> Red Hat Enterprise Linux 8 (64-bit) or Oracle Linux 8.</li> <li><strong>vCPU:</strong> Size per Oracle workload (8–32 vCPU typical)</li> <li><strong>Memory:</strong> 32–256 GiB based on SGA/PGA requirements.</li> <li><strong>Disk:</strong> 100 GiB on vSAN datastore (OS + Oracle Home + swap + temp tablespace)</li> <li><strong>Network:</strong> Attach to DB segment on the prod-trusted Tier-1 gateway.</li> </ul></li> <li>Power on the VM and configure the guest OS:</li> </ol> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Set hostname hostnamectl set-hostname ora-db1 # Create swap on vSAN-backed disk (local NVMe, single-digit ms latency) # The vSAN datastore is already available as the VM's primary disk # Allocate a dedicated partition or LV for swap lvcreate -L 16G -n swap vgos mkswap /dev/vgos/swap swapon /dev/vgos/swap echo "/dev/vgos/swap swap swap defaults 0 0" &gt;&gt; /etc/fstab # Install Oracle prerequisites sudo yum install -y oracle-database-preinstall-19c python3</code></pre> </div> <p>For HA/DR, consider replicating the entire Oracle VM rather than maintaining a separate licensed instance in the DR cluster (see DR Licensing Consideration in the architecture post).</p> <blockquote> <p><strong>Oracle licensing consideration:</strong> Instance type selection affects Oracle license cost, which is an important factor to take into consideration. We recommend requesting an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/optimization-and-licensing-assessment/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Optimization and Licensing Assessment (AWS OLA)</a> for further guidelines.</p> </blockquote> <hr> <h3 id="step-2-provision-fsx-for-netapp-ontap">Step 2: Provision FSx for NetApp ONTAP</h3> <ol type="1"> <li>Open the Amazon FSx console, select <strong>Create file system</strong>, then select <strong>Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP</strong>.</li> <li>Select <strong>Standard create</strong> and configure:</li> </ol> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Setting</strong></td> <td><strong>Value</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Deployment type</td> <td>Single-AZ (required for EVS)</td> </tr> <tr> <td>SSD storage capacity</td> <td>Size for Oracle data + logs + 20% headroom</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Throughput capacity</td> <td>512–2,048 MB/s (size for write workload. See asymmetry note)</td> </tr> <tr> <td>IOPS</td> <td>Automatic (3/GiB) or user-provisioned (up to 80,000)</td> </tr> <tr> <td>VPC</td> <td>Same VPC as EVS environment</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Subnet</td> <td>EVS service access subnet, same AZ as DB cluster</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Security group</td> <td>Allow NFS (TCP 2049, 111, 635) from EVS management VLAN</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <ol start="3" type="1"> <li>Set the <code>fsxadmin</code> password (required for ONTAP CLI automation).</li> <li>Create an SVM (Storage Virtual Machine) with <code>vsadmin</code> password.</li> <li>Disable automatic daily backups. Use SnapCenter for Oracle-aware scheduling instead.</li> <li>After creation, select SVM, select <strong>Endpoints</strong>, and copy the NFS DNS name.</li> </ol> <hr> <h3 id="step-3-create-oracle-database-volumes">Step 3: Create Oracle database volumes</h3> <p>Connect to the FSx for ONTAP cluster using SSH (<code>ssh fsxadmin@management-endpoint</code>) and create volumes:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Oracle binary volume vol create -volume oradb1bin -aggregate aggr1 -size 50G \ -state online -policy default -tiering-policy none \ -junction-path /oradb1bin # Oracle data volume vol create -volume oradb1data -aggregate aggr1 -size 500G \ -state online -policy default -tiering-policy none \ -junction-path /oradb1data # Oracle log volume (redo + archive) vol create -volume oradb1log -aggregate aggr1 -size 250G \ -state online -policy default -tiering-policy none \ -junction-path /oradb1log</code></pre> </div> <p>Set <code>-tiering-policy none</code> to pin all data to SSD tier. Size the log volume for 24 hours of archive logs.</p> <hr> <h3 id="step-4-mount-fsx-for-ontap-as-nfs-datastore-in-vsphere">Step 4: Mount FSx for ONTAP as NFS datastore in vSphere</h3> <ol type="1"> <li>In vSphere Client, select the DB Cluster, then select <strong>Configure</strong> &gt; <strong>Storage</strong> &gt; <strong>New Datastore</strong>.</li> <li>Select <strong>NFS</strong>, then select <strong>NFS 3</strong>.</li> <li>Enter: <ul> <li><strong>Server:</strong> <code>svm-id.fs-id.fsx.region.amazonaws.com</code></li> <li><strong>Folder:</strong> <code>/oradb1data</code></li> <li><strong>Datastore name:</strong> <code>fsx-ora-db1-data</code></li> </ul></li> <li>Repeat for binary (<code>/oradb1bin</code>) and log (<code>/oradb1log</code>) volumes.</li> <li>Verify all three datastores show correct capacity in the cluster storage view.</li> </ol> <hr> <h3 id="step-5-create-oracle-vmdks-on-fsx-for-ontap-datastores">Step 5: Create Oracle VMDKs on FSx for ONTAP datastores</h3> <p>With the NFS datastores mounted at the ESXi host level (Step 4), create virtual disks for Oracle on these datastores:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>In vSphere Client, select the Oracle VM, select <strong>Edit Settings</strong>, then select <strong>Add New Device</strong> &gt; <strong>Hard Disk</strong>.</li> <li>Create these VMDKs:</li> </ol> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>VMDK</strong></td> <td><strong>Datastore</strong></td> <td><strong>Size</strong></td> <td><strong>Guest Mount</strong></td> <td><strong>Purpose</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Hard Disk 2</td> <td><code>fsx-ora-db1-data</code></td> <td>500 GiB</td> <td><code>/u02</code></td> <td>Oracle data files</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Hard Disk 3</td> <td><code>fsx-ora-db1-log</code></td> <td>250 GiB</td> <td><code>/u03</code></td> <td>Oracle redo + archive logs</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Hard Disk 4</td> <td><code>fsx-ora-db1-bin</code></td> <td>50 GiB</td> <td><code>/u01</code></td> <td>Oracle Home binaries</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <ol start="3" type="1"> <li>Select <strong>Thick Provision, Eager Zeroed</strong> for data and log VMDKs (best Oracle performance).</li> </ol> <p>Oracle Database can be created on Oracle ASM or Filesystem (local/NFS). This installation is based on creating the Oracle database on local XFS filesystem.</p> <p>Inside the Oracle VM guest OS, partition and mount the new disks:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Identify new disks lsblk # Create filesystem on each disk (example: /dev/sdb for data) mkfs.xfs /dev/sdb mkfs.xfs /dev/sdc mkfs.xfs /dev/sdd # Create mount points mkdir -p /u01 /u02 /u03 # Mount mount /dev/sdd /u01 # Oracle Home (binaries) mount /dev/sdb /u02 # Oracle data files mount /dev/sdc /u03 # Oracle redo + archive logs # Persist in /etc/fstab cat &gt;&gt; /etc/fstab &lt;&lt;EOF /dev/sdb /u02 xfs defaults,noatime 0 0 /dev/sdc /u03 xfs defaults,noatime 0 0 /dev/sdd /u01 xfs defaults,noatime 0 0 EOF # Set ownership chown -R oracle:oinstall /u01 /u02 /u03</code></pre> </div> <p><strong>Key insight:</strong> The Oracle VM accesses <code>/u02</code> and <code>/u03</code> as local XFS block devices. It has no awareness that the underlying storage is an NFS datastore backed by FSx for ONTAP. All NFS communication happens at the ESXi host level, where each host uses its own network path to FSx for ONTAP.</p> <hr> <h3 id="step-6-install-and-configure-oracle-19c">Step 6: Install and configure Oracle 19c</h3> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># As oracle user export ORACLE_HOME=/u01/app/oracle/product/19.0.0/dbhome_1 cd $ORACLE_HOME ./runInstaller -silent -responseFile /path/to/db_install.rsp</code></pre> </div> <p>Create the database with data on <code>/u02</code> and logs on <code>/u03</code>:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-sql">CREATE DATABASE orcl DATAFILE '/u02/oradata/orcl/system01.dbf' SIZE 1G LOGFILE GROUP 1 '/u03/oralogs/orcl/redo01.log' SIZE 512M, GROUP 2 '/u03/oralogs/orcl/redo02.log' SIZE 512M, GROUP 3 '/u03/oralogs/orcl/redo03.log' SIZE 512M;</code></pre> </div> <p>Oracle accesses <code>/u02</code> and <code>/u03</code> as local XFS filesystems. Standard Oracle ASM or filesystem-based storage management applies. No NFS-specific Oracle configuration is needed because the NFS layer is abstracted by the ESXi hypervisor.</p> <hr> <h3 id="step-7-set-up-snapmirror-for-cross-region-dr">Step 7: Set up SnapMirror for cross-region DR</h3> <p><strong>Peer clusters (production → DR):</strong></p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">cluster peer create -peer-addrs &lt;dr-cluster-intercluster-ip&gt; \ -username fsxadmin -initial-allowed-vserver-peers *</code></pre> </div> <p><strong>Peer SVMs:</strong></p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">vserver peer create -vserver svm-prod -peer-vserver svm-dr \ -peer-cluster FSxDR -applications snapmirror</code></pre> </div> <p><strong>Create DP volumes on DR FSx for ONTAP:</strong></p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">vol create -volume oradb1bin -aggregate aggr1 -size 50G -state online -type DP vol create -volume oradb1data -aggregate aggr1 -size 500G -state online -type DP vol create -volume oradb1log -aggregate aggr1 -size 250G -state online -type DP</code></pre> </div> <p><strong>Create and initialize SnapMirror:</strong></p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">snapmirror create -source-path svm-prod:oradb1data \ -destination-path svm-dr:oradb1data -throttle unlimited \ -policy MirrorAllSnapshots -type DP snapmirror create -source-path svm-prod:oradb1log \ -destination-path svm-dr:oradb1log -throttle unlimited \ -policy MirrorAllSnapshots -type DP snapmirror create -source-path svm-prod:oradb1bin \ -destination-path svm-dr:oradb1bin -throttle unlimited \ -policy MirrorAllSnapshots -type DP # Initialize snapmirror initialize -destination-path svm-dr:oradb1data snapmirror initialize -destination-path svm-dr:oradb1log snapmirror initialize -destination-path svm-dr:oradb1bin</code></pre> </div> <blockquote> <p><strong>Important:</strong> Consider Oracle licensing requirements when planning your DR strategy. An alternative is to replicate the Oracle VM through NetApp SnapMirror from Production to DR. Keep the DR replicated volumes as data-protection (DP) volumes that are NOT mounted as NFS datastores on DR hosts until a failover event is declared to avoid Oracle double licensing. Only then break the SnapMirror, mount the NFS datastore on the DR Host, and power on the VM. Pre-mounting the SnapMirror volume as a datastore — even with no VM powered on — means Oracle binaries are accessible on those hosts, which Oracle may consider an “installation” requiring licenses across the entire DR cluster. We recommend requesting an AWS Optimization and Licensing Assessment (AWS OLA) for further guidelines.</p> </blockquote> <hr> <h3 id="step-8-configure-snapcenter-backup">Step 8: Configure SnapCenter backup</h3> <ol type="1"> <li>Deploy SnapCenter Server (or use SnapCenter SaaS).</li> <li>Add FSx for ONTAP storage system using the cluster management IP.</li> <li>Install SnapCenter Plugin for Oracle on each Oracle VM.</li> <li>Create backup policies:</li> </ol> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Policy</strong></td> <td><strong>Scope</strong></td> <td><strong>Frequency</strong></td> <td><strong>SnapMirror Update</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Full DB Backup</td> <td>Data + Control + Archive</td> <td>Every 4–6 hours</td> <td>Yes</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Archive Log</td> <td>Archive logs only</td> <td>Every 10–15 minutes</td> <td>Yes</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <ol start="5" type="1"> <li>Create resource groups, assign policies, and schedule.</li> </ol> <hr> <h2 id="database-migration-from-on-premises-vmware-to-evs">Database migration from on-premises VMware to EVS</h2> <h3 id="option-1-vmware-hcx-live-migration">Option 1: VMware HCX live migration</h3> <p>For enterprises with existing VMware on-premises:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Deploy HCX Connector on-premises, HCX Cloud Manager on EVS.</li> <li>Create site pairing and network extensions (L2 stretch).</li> <li>Migrate Oracle VMs using HCX vMotion (zero downtime) or Bulk Migration.</li> <li>Post-migration: storage vMotion Oracle VMDKs from vSAN to FSx for ONTAP NFS datastores for snapshot/replication capabilities.</li> </ol> <h3 id="option-2-snapmirror-ontap-to-ontap">Option 2: SnapMirror ONTAP-to-ONTAP</h3> <p>If on-premises Oracle already uses NetApp ONTAP storage:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Establish SnapMirror between on-premises ONTAP and AWS FSx for ONTAP.</li> <li>Incrementally replicate until cutover.</li> <li>At switchover: quiesce Oracle, flush archive logs, final SnapMirror sync, break mirror.</li> <li>Mount FSx for ONTAP volumes on EVS Oracle VM, recover database, open for service.</li> </ol> <h3 id="option-3-oracle-pdb-relocation-multitenant">Option 3: Oracle PDB relocation (multitenant)</h3> <p>For Oracle databases already in PDB/CDB multitenant model:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Create target CDB on EVS with FSx for ONTAP storage.</li> <li>Use PDB hot clone to relocate PDBs from on-premises CDB to AWS CDB.</li> <li>Minimal service interruption. Only final switchover requires brief outage.</li> </ol> <h3 id="option-4-rman-backuprestore-non-ontap-on-premises">Option 4: RMAN backup/restore (non-ONTAP on-premises)</h3> <p>If Oracle runs on non-ONTAP storage on-premises:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Create RMAN backup, stage to <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)</a> using <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/datasync/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS DataSync</a> or AWS Direct Connect.</li> <li>Provision Oracle VM on EVS, mount FSx for ONTAP volumes.</li> <li>Restore from RMAN backup, apply archive logs.</li> <li>Open database and redirect applications.</li> </ol> <hr> <h2 id="day-2-operations">Day-2 operations</h2> <h3 id="snapshot-backup">Snapshot backup</h3> <p>SnapCenter manages full database snapshots as storage-layer operations, providing efficient backup capabilities.</p> <h3 id="point-in-time-recovery">Point-in-time recovery</h3> <p>In SnapCenter, select the SCN or timestamp, mount the log snapshot, restore the data snapshot, apply archive logs, and open with RESETLOGS.</p> <h3 id="database-cloning">Database cloning</h3> <p>SnapCenter FlexClone creates space-efficient database copies. Clones share unchanged blocks with the source and consume storage only for deltas. Use for dev/test, patch validation, and reporting.</p> <h3 id="ha-failover-procedure">HA failover procedure</h3> <ol type="1"> <li>Break SnapMirror on DR volumes.</li> <li>Mount SnapMirror volumes as NFS datastores on DR ESXi hosts, then power on the Oracle VM.</li> <li>Recover to last available archive log.</li> <li>Open database. Update DNS/connection strings.</li> </ol> <hr> <h2 id="clean-up">Clean up</h2> <p>To stop incurring charges after testing this deployment, remove the following resources in this order:</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Oracle VMs</strong> — Power off and delete Oracle database VMs from the vSphere inventory.</li> <li><strong>NFS datastores</strong> — Unmount FSx for ONTAP datastores from ESXi hosts in vSphere.</li> <li><strong>SnapMirror relationships</strong> — Delete SnapMirror relationships and DP volumes on the DR FSx for ONTAP file system.</li> <li><strong>FSx for ONTAP file systems</strong> — Delete both production and DR file systems from the Amazon FSx console. This action deletes all volumes and data on those file systems.</li> <li><strong>Amazon EVS environment</strong> — Delete the EVS environment from the Amazon EVS console. This terminates the underlying EC2 bare metal instances.</li> <li><strong>Networking</strong> — Remove Transit Gateway attachments, VPC Route Server configurations, and Direct Connect connections if they were created solely for this deployment.</li> </ol> <blockquote> <p><strong>Important:</strong> Deleting an FSx for ONTAP file system permanently removes all data. Confirm that you have backed up any data you need before proceeding.</p> </blockquote> <hr> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>In this post, we walked through deploying Oracle Database on Amazon Elastic VMware Service (Amazon EVS) with Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP as NFS datastore storage. You provisioned the Oracle VM, created FSx for ONTAP volumes, mounted NFS datastores in vSphere, installed Oracle 19c, and configured SnapMirror for cross-region disaster recovery and SnapCenter for Oracle-aware backup. We also covered four migration paths for existing on-premises Oracle workloads and day-2 operations for backup, point-in-time recovery, and cloning.</p> <p>To get started, review the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/evs/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EVS User Guide</a> and <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/fsx/latest/ONTAPGuide/evs-nfs-datastore.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Configure FSx for ONTAP as NFS Datastore for EVS</a>, then deploy your first Oracle VM on Amazon EVS. For the architecture decisions behind this deployment, see our related post, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/architect-highly-available-oracle-database-on-amazon-evs-and-fsx-for-ontap/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Architect highly available Oracle Database on Amazon EVS and FSx for ONTAP</a>. Share your feedback and questions in the comments.</p> <hr> <h2 id="additional-resources">Additional resources</h2> <ul> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/evs/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EVS User Guide</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/evs/latest/userguide/supported-versions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">EVS Supported VCF Versions and Instance Types</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/fsx/latest/ONTAPGuide/evs-nfs-datastore.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Configure FSx for ONTAP as NFS Datastore for EVS</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/fsx/latest/ONTAPGuide/evs.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">FSx for NetApp ONTAP with EVS</a></li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/evs/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EVS Pricing</a></li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/introducing-amazon-elastic-vmware-service/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Introducing Amazon EVS — AWS Blog</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.netapp.com/us-en/netapp-solutions/databases/automation_ora_anf_nfs.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">NetApp Automation Toolkit for Oracle</a></li> </ul> <p style="clear: both"></p> <hr style="width: 100%"> <h2>About the authors</h2>

Architect highly available Oracle Database on Amazon EVS and FSx for ONTAP

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/architect-highly-available-oracle-database-on-amazon-evs-and-fsx-for-ontap/📅 2026-10-03 01:57 UTC+8

<hr> <p>Enterprises with existing VMware Cloud Foundation (VCF) investments want to migrate their Oracle databases to AWS without rearchitecting applications or retraining operations teams. Oracle on <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/evs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Elastic VMware Service (Amazon EVS)</a> can take advantage of sub-millisecond storage latency, snapshot-based backup, cross-region disaster recovery (DR), and independent storage scaling, all while preserving existing VMware operational workflows.</p> <p>In this post, we show you how to architect a complete Oracle Database environment on Amazon EVS with <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fsx/netapp-ontap/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP</a>. You learn how the storage, compute, networking, and disaster recovery layers work together to deliver high availability, cross-region DR, and sub-millisecond storage latency while maintaining your familiar VMware operational tooling.</p> <p><strong>In this post, you learn how to:</strong></p> <ul> <li>Design Oracle Database architecture on Amazon EVS running VMware Cloud Foundation 9.1.</li> <li>Select optimal EC2 bare metal instance types and VM sizing for Oracle workloads.</li> <li>Architect storage using Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP as NFS datastores for Oracle data and log volumes.</li> <li>Plan SnapMirror replication for cross-region disaster recovery.</li> <li>Evaluate migration options for moving existing Oracle workloads from on-premises VMware to EVS.</li> </ul> <p>Amazon EVS directly runs VMware Cloud Foundation (VCF) environments on <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2)</a> bare metal instances within an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC)</a>. With VCF 9.x, Amazon EVS provisions the bare metal infrastructure and VLAN subnets, and you then deploy VCF using Broadcom’s VCF Installer (the self-deployed model). VCF 9.x also supports evaluation mode, so you can validate the design before applying license keys, and the Solutions for Amazon EVS GitHub repository provides CloudFormation and Terraform templates to automate the phased VCF 9 deployment. Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP provides managed ONTAP storage with NFS, SMB, iSCSI, and NVMe over TCP access. FSx for NetApp ONTAP can deliver sub-millisecond response times, multiple GBps of throughput, and up to 80,000 IOPS per file system (see <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/fsx/latest/ONTAPGuide/performance.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">FSx for ONTAP performance</a>).</p> <p>For more information, see <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fsx/netapp-ontap/features/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">FSx for NetApp ONTAP features</a>.</p> <p>For step-by-step deployment procedures including provisioning, storage configuration, Oracle installation, and SnapMirror setup, see our companion post: <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/deploy-oracle-database-step-by-step-on-amazon-evs-with-fsx-for-ontap/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Deploy Oracle Database step by step on Amazon EVS with FSx for ONTAP</a>.</p> <hr> <h2 id="solution-architecture">Solution architecture</h2> <p>This section describes a highly available Oracle Database deployment on Amazon EVS with Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP storage across two AWS Regions. Figure 1 illustrates the numbered data flow from on-premises through hybrid connectivity into the production EVS environment and cross-region DR.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/17/ARCHBLOG-1652-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/18/arch-1652-fig-1.png" alt="Oracle on Amazon EVS reference architecture showing on-premises to AWS connectivity, the production EVS cluster on FSx for ONTAP, and cross-region SnapMirror DR" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1: Oracle Database on Amazon EVS with FSx for NetApp ONTAP and cross-region SnapMirror DR</p> </div> <p>The architecture consists of eight functional components, described in the following section.</p> <h3 id="architecture-components">Architecture components</h3> <p>These numbered items correspond to the data flow shown in the diagram.</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Hybrid link</strong> — On-premises data center connects to AWS through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/directconnect/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Direct Connect</a> for dedicated, low-latency bandwidth.</li> <li><strong>Transit routing</strong> — <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/transit-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Transit Gateway</a> routes traffic between the production VPC, DR region, and on-premises networks.</li> <li><strong>Workload landing</strong> — Traffic reaches the EVS Database Cluster running on i7i.metal-24xl Amazon EC2 bare metal instances with VCF 9.1.</li> <li><strong>Live migration</strong> — VMware HCX (Hybrid Cloud Extension) migrates Oracle VMs from on-premises VMware to EVS with near-zero downtime using Replication Assisted vMotion or bulk migration.</li> <li><strong>NFS data path</strong> — Each ESXi host mounts Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP as an NFS datastore. Oracle VMs access database volumes as block devices (VMDKs on the NFS datastore) with sub-millisecond latency and up to 80,000 IOPS. Each host has its own independent NFS path to FSx for ONTAP, distributing bandwidth across the cluster.</li> <li><strong>Cross-region DR</strong> — SnapMirror asynchronously replicates FSx for ONTAP volumes (data, logs, binaries) to the DR region with configurable Recovery Point Objective (RPO).</li> <li><strong>Dynamic routing</strong> — NSX Tier-0 gateway peers through BGP with <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/vpc/latest/userguide/route-server.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon VPC Route Server</a>. This is one-way BGP. Route Server listens for routes advertised by NSX and writes them to the VPC route table, but does not advertise VPC routes back to NSX.</li> <li><strong>DR failover</strong> — On failover, Transit Gateway routes to the DR region where pre-provisioned standby Oracle VMs mount the SnapMirror replica volumes.</li> </ol> <hr> <h2 id="factors-to-consider-for-oracle-database-deployment-on-evs">Factors to consider for Oracle Database deployment on EVS</h2> <p>Before you begin deployment, evaluate your Oracle workload requirements against the available infrastructure options. The decisions you make for instance types, VM sizing, and storage architecture directly affect database performance, cost, and operational complexity.</p> <blockquote> <p><strong>Oracle licensing consideration:</strong> Instance type selection affects Oracle license cost, which is an important factor to take into consideration. We recommend requesting an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/optimization-and-licensing-assessment/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Optimization and Licensing Assessment (AWS OLA)</a> for further guidelines.</p> </blockquote> <h3 id="ec2-instance-type-selection-for-esxi-hosts">EC2 instance type selection for ESXi hosts</h3> <p>Amazon EVS currently supports three bare metal instance types for ESXi hosts. The choice depends on whether you prioritize per-core compute speed (i7i) or per-host memory and storage density (i4i), and on how much you want to scale a single host vertically. For most new Oracle deployments, <strong>i7i is the better choice</strong> because Oracle query performance is sensitive to CPU instruction throughput and storage IO latency.</p> <ul> <li><strong>i7i.metal-24xl</strong> (recommended default): 96 vCPUs (48 cores), 768 GiB RAM. 5th Gen Intel Xeon delivers improved compute performance, critical for Oracle CPU-bound queries. 3rd Gen AWS Nitro SSDs provide enhanced real-time storage performance and lower IO latency for vSAN.</li> <li><strong>i7i.metal-48xl</strong>: Same 5th Gen Intel Xeon as the 24xl but double the capacity per host (192 vCPUs, 96 cores, 1,536 GiB RAM, up to 100 Gbps network and 60 Gbps Amazon Elastic Block Store (Amazon EBS) bandwidth). Choose this when you want to scale a single Oracle host vertically, such as for large SGAs, high core counts, or fewer and denser hosts to reduce VMware per-host licensing. It keeps the same per-core performance profile as the 24xl.</li> <li><strong>i4i.metal</strong>: Higher per-host density (128 vCPUs, 1,024 GiB RAM, 30 TB NVMe) suits environments requiring fewer, larger hosts to reduce VMware licensing costs.</li> </ul> <p><strong>VCF compatibility:</strong> All three instance types support VCF 9.1 with ESXi 9.1 (build 9.1.0.0100.25433460). VCF 5.2.2 (ESXi 8.0U3g) remains available but is on a path to end of support, so new deployments should default to 9.x. An EVS environment supports 4–32 hosts per cluster. You can mix instance types across clusters within the same SDDC.</p> <h3 id="vm-sizing-for-oracle-database-guests">VM sizing for Oracle Database guests</h3> <p>Size Oracle Database VMs based on these workload characteristics:</p> <ul> <li>Allocate vCPU count matching Oracle CPU_COUNT parameter.</li> <li>Size memory for SGA + PGA + OS overhead (typically 75–85% of allocated VM memory for SGA)</li> <li>Configure VM swap on vSAN datastore (not NFS). vSAN uses local NVMe with single-digit millisecond latency.</li> <li>Use NUMA-aware VM placement for VMs exceeding single-socket core count.</li> <li>Place OS swap and Oracle temp tablespace on the vSAN datastore for single digit ms latency at no additional cost.</li> </ul> <h3 id="storage-architecture-vsan-fsx-for-netapp-ontap">Storage architecture: vSAN + FSx for NetApp ONTAP</h3> <p>The recommended design splits storage responsibilities between two tiers:</p> <ul> <li><strong>vSAN</strong> (backed by local NVMe drives on each ESXi host) handles low-latency, non-replicated workloads: VM boot disks, OS swap, and Oracle temp tablespace.</li> <li><strong>FSx for NetApp ONTAP</strong> handles Oracle data files and redo logs that require snapshot-based backup and cross-region replication. ESXi hosts mount FSx for ONTAP volumes as NFS datastores, and Oracle VMs access standard VMDKs on those datastores.</li> </ul> <p>This separation gives local NVMe speed for transient IO while adding snapshot, clone, and SnapMirror capabilities for persistent database files.</p> <p><strong>Why NFS datastore (host-level) instead of in-guest NFS (dNFS)?</strong> With in-guest NFS, all Oracle NFS traffic routes through the NSX overlay and an NSX Edge node before reaching FSx for ONTAP. This creates a single Edge chokepoint. With NFS datastores, each ESXi host talks NFS directly to FSx for ONTAP using its own network bandwidth. There is no Edge bottleneck and no extra latency hop.</p> <h3 id="fsx-for-netapp-ontap-sizing-considerations">FSx for NetApp ONTAP sizing considerations</h3> <blockquote> <p><strong>Important:</strong> Use 100% SSD for Oracle. We recommend against using capacity pool tiering for Oracle database volumes. Keep tiering policy set to <code>none</code> for all Oracle volumes.</p> <p><strong>Important:</strong> SSD capacity planning. If the SSD tier fills to capacity, FSx for ONTAP blocks writes. Monitor SSD utilization and provision headroom (minimum 20% free).</p> <p><strong>Important:</strong> Read/write throughput asymmetry. On a 6 GB/s filesystem, read throughput can reach 6 GB/s, but write throughput is limited to approximately 1 GB/s. Size throughput capacity based on Oracle write workload requirements. This write ceiling applies per high-availability (HA) pair. To scale write throughput beyond a single HA pair, deploy a file system with more than one HA pair and distribute Oracle volumes across the additional aggregates so writes are spread across pairs. This placement is not automatic, so plan the layout up front to keep write-heavy datasets balanced.</p> </blockquote> <hr> <h2 id="network-architecture">Network architecture</h2> <p>Amazon EVS uses VLAN subnets (defined at environment creation and unchangeable later) to segment traffic.</p> <p>VPC Route Server replaces static routes within the VPC. NSX Tier-0 gateways peer through BGP with Route Server endpoints. This is one-way BGP: Route Server listens for routes from NSX and programs them into the VPC route table but does not advertise VPC routes back to NSX. Beyond the VPC, routes remain static at Transit Gateway.</p> <h3 id="nsx-t-segmentation-for-oracle">NSX-T segmentation for Oracle</h3> <ul> <li>Dedicated Tier-1 gateway for production database segments (DB subnets)</li> <li>Separate Tier-1 for application tier (App subnets) and perimeter network.</li> <li>Distributed firewall rules restrict Oracle listener access (TCP 1521) to authorized application segments only.</li> <li>Micro-segmentation between Oracle instances prevents lateral movement.</li> </ul> <blockquote> <p><strong>Important:</strong> Security group rules are not enforced on VLAN subnet interfaces. Use network ACLs and NSX distributed firewall for traffic control.</p> </blockquote> <hr> <h2 id="high-availability-and-disaster-recovery">High availability and disaster recovery</h2> <ul> <li>SnapMirror replication frequency determines RPO. Configure based on business requirements.</li> <li>Pre-provision standby Oracle VMs in the DR cluster to reduce Recovery Time Objective (RTO).</li> <li>Replicate binary volumes so that Oracle installation is not required during recovery.</li> <li>Automate failover with Ansible/SnapCenter to reduce human error.</li> </ul> <blockquote> <p><strong>Oracle licensing consideration for DR:</strong> There are license impacts based on how DR replication is implemented. If you have licensing questions, we recommend requesting an AWS Optimization and Licensing Assessment (AWS OLA).</p> <p>To comply with the Oracle licensing rules, an alternative is to replicate the Oracle VM through NetApp SnapMirror from Production to DR. Keep the DR replicated volumes as data-protection (DP) volumes that are NOT mounted as NFS datastores on DR hosts until a failover event is declared to avoid Oracle double licensing. Only then break the SnapMirror, mount the NFS datastore on the DR Host, and power on the VM. Pre-mounting the SnapMirror volume as a datastore, even with no VM powered on, means Oracle binaries are accessible on those hosts, which Oracle may consider an “installation” requiring licenses across the entire DR cluster.</p> </blockquote> <hr> <h2 id="database-migration-from-on-premises-vmware-to-evs">Database migration from on-premises VMware to EVS</h2> <p>The following table compares migration options from on-premises VMware to EVS.</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Option</strong></td> <td><strong>Method</strong></td> <td><strong>Best for</strong></td> <td><strong>Downtime</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>1</td> <td>VMware HCX Live Migration</td> <td>Existing VMware on-prem</td> <td>Near-zero (vMotion) or planned bulk</td> </tr> <tr> <td>2</td> <td>SnapMirror <code>ONTAP-to-ONTAP</code></td> <td>On-prem Oracle on NetApp ONTAP</td> <td>Minutes (final sync switchover)</td> </tr> <tr> <td>3</td> <td>Oracle PDB Relocation</td> <td>PDB/CDB multitenant model</td> <td>Brief (final switchover only)</td> </tr> <tr> <td>4</td> <td>RMAN Backup/Restore</td> <td>Non-ONTAP on-prem (universal)</td> <td>Hours (backup + restore + apply)</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p>For detailed procedures on each migration option, see our companion post: <a href="https://repost.aws/articles/AR3bnQsABSSIS0J8pO8mIUCA/oracle-database-deployment-on-amazon-evs-and-fsx-for-netapp-ontap-best-practices" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Deploy Oracle Database step by step on Amazon EVS with FSx for ONTAP</a>.</p> <hr> <h2 id="security">Security</h2> <p>Security for Oracle on Amazon EVS spans multiple layers from network isolation to database-level encryption. The following table summarizes the security controls across each layer.</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Layer</strong></td> <td><strong>Control</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Network segmentation</td> <td>NSX-T Tier-1 gateways isolate DB/App/perimeter network segments</td> </tr> <tr> <td>East-west traffic</td> <td>NSX Distributed Firewall: restrict TCP 1521 to authorized app segments</td> </tr> <tr> <td>North-south traffic</td> <td>FortiGate or equivalent inspection VPC for ingress/egress filtering</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Encryption at rest</td> <td>FSx for ONTAP volumes encrypted with <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kms/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS)</a></td> </tr> <tr> <td>Encryption in transit</td> <td>VPC encryption for NFS traffic, and IPsec for SnapMirror cross-region</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Database encryption</td> <td>Oracle TDE (Transparent Data Encryption) for additional protection</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Administrative access</td> <td>Zero-trust access (for example, Banyan or Zscaler) for VMware admin consoles</td> </tr> <tr> <td>VLAN subnet security</td> <td>Network ACLs (security groups not enforced on VLAN interfaces)</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <hr> <h2 id="cost-optimization">Cost optimization</h2> <p>Cost optimization for Oracle on Amazon EVS focuses on matching infrastructure capacity to workload demands and using AWS pricing models. The following table summarizes key strategies across compute, storage, networking, and Oracle licensing. We recommend requesting an AWS Optimization and Licensing Assessment (AWS OLA) for further guidelines.</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Component</strong></td> <td><strong>Strategy</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>EC2 bare metal hosts</td> <td><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/savingsplans/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Compute Savings Plans</a> or <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/pricing/reserved-instances/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Reserved Instances</a> (up to 54% savings)</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Instance type selection</td> <td>i7i.metal-24xl delivers ~10% price-performance over i4i.metal</td> </tr> <tr> <td>FSx for ONTAP throughput</td> <td>Right-size for the write workload, and adjust on the fly</td> </tr> <tr> <td>FSx for ONTAP storage</td> <td>100% SSD for Oracle, and storage efficiency for non-production</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Data transfer</td> <td>Place FSx for ONTAP in same AZ as EVS cluster</td> </tr> <tr> <td>SnapMirror replication</td> <td>Schedule frequency based on RPO (less frequent = lower cost)</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Oracle DR licensing</td> <td>Replicate the Oracle VMs through NetApp SnapMirror from Production to DR, keeping the DR replicated volumes as data-protection (DP) volumes that are NOT mounted as NFS datastores on DR hosts until a failover event is declared to avoid Oracle double licensing. When a failover event is declared, then break the SnapMirror, mount the NFS datastore on the DR Host, and power on the VM. We recommend requesting an AWS Optimization and Licensing Assessment (AWS OLA) for further guidelines.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>Non-production</td> <td>Use fewer hosts, and apply tiering for dev/test data</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <hr> <h2 id="summary">Summary</h2> <p>Deploying Oracle databases on Amazon EVS with Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP provides high availability, cross-region DR, and sub-millisecond storage latency while combining VMware operational consistency with AWS cloud economics:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Performance:</strong> i7i.metal-24xl delivers up to 23% better compute and 50% lower IO latency. FSx for ONTAP delivers sub-millisecond latency with up to 80,000 IOPS. For details, see <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2025/10/amazon-ec2-i7i-instances-aws-govcloud-us-regions/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EC2 i7i instances in the AWS GovCloud (US) Regions</a>.</li> <li><strong>Availability:</strong> vSphere HA, SnapMirror cross-region replication, and optional Oracle Data Guard.</li> <li><strong>Manageability:</strong> SnapCenter for backup, clone, and recovery in seconds regardless of database size.</li> <li><strong>Migration flexibility:</strong> HCX (live), SnapMirror (<code>ONTAP-to-ONTAP</code>), PDB Relocation (multi-tenant), and RMAN (universal).</li> <li><strong>Security:</strong> NSX micro-segmentation, AWS KMS encryption, and zero-trust access.</li> <li><strong>Cost efficiency:</strong> Improved price performance with i7i, on-the-fly throughput adjustment, and AWS Savings Plans.</li> </ul> <p>This architecture provides you with high availability, cross-region DR, and storage-based backup and cloning similar to Oracle RAC and Data Guard functions while maintaining your familiar VMware operational tooling and procedures.</p> <hr> <h2 id="next-steps">Next steps</h2> <p>To get started with this deployment:</p> <ol type="1"> <li>Provision an Amazon EVS environment in your target Region. See the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/evs/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EVS User Guide</a> for setup instructions.</li> <li>Deploy an Amazon FSx for NetApp ONTAP file system in the same VPC and Availability Zone as your EVS cluster.</li> <li>Follow the step-by-step procedures in our companion post to mount NFS datastores, create Oracle VMDKs, and configure SnapMirror DR.</li> <li>Test in a non-production environment first, then migrate production Oracle workloads.</li> </ol> <hr> <h2 id="additional-resources">Additional resources</h2> <ul> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/evs/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EVS User Guide</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/evs/latest/userguide/supported-versions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">EVS Supported VCF Versions and Instance Types</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/fsx/latest/ONTAPGuide/evs-nfs-datastore.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Configure FSx for ONTAP as NFS Datastore for EVS</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/fsx/latest/ONTAPGuide/evs.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">FSx for NetApp ONTAP with EVS</a></li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/evs/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EVS Pricing</a></li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/aws/introducing-amazon-elastic-vmware-service/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Introducing Amazon EVS on the AWS News Blog</a></li> <li><a href="https://docs.netapp.com/us-en/netapp-solutions/databases/automation_ora_anf_nfs.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">NetApp Automation Toolkit for Oracle</a></li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/deploy-oracle-database-step-by-step-on-amazon-evs-with-fsx-for-ontap/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Deploy Oracle Database step by step on Amazon EVS with FSx for ONTAP</a> (companion post)</li> </ul> <p style="clear: both"></p> <hr style="width: 100%"> <h2>About the authors</h2>

Deploy open source Regional availability tools in your VPC

https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/architecture/deploy-open-source-regional-availability-tools-in-your-vpc/📅 2026-10-03 01:50 UTC+8

<p>When you build multi-Region architectures on AWS, one question comes up early: “What services are available in each AWS Region?” The answer shapes architecture decisions, from which AWS Regions to expand into, to how you design for resilience. For teams navigating data residency requirements and compliance reporting, getting the answer wrong has real consequences: deployment failures, compliance gaps, and delayed launches.</p> <p>AWS publishes Regional availability data covering services, features, APIs, and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudformation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS CloudFormation</a> resource types across all AWS Regions. You can explore this data on the <a href="https://builder.aws.com/build/capabilities/explore?tab=service-feature" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Capabilities by Region</a> page, access it through <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)</a> for pipeline integration, or query it using the <a href="https://awslabs.github.io/mcp/servers/aws-knowledge-mcp-server/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Knowledge MCP server</a>. These options work well for exploration and automation. However, teams told us they need this data deployed as infrastructure they own, refreshing on their schedule, inside their network, filtered to their workload. That means running availability data the same way you run the rest of your stack: in your <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC)</a>, under your governance.</p> <p>In this post, we introduce two open source solutions that deliver that ownership. <a href="https://github.com/aws/capability-insights-for-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Capability Insights for AWS</a> deploys a Regional availability dashboard into your VPC that auto-refreshes every 24 hours. The dashboard, its API, and availability data all run in your own account, and the scheduled refresh makes the only call that leaves your account when it reads the dataset that AWS publishes. <a href="https://github.com/aws/capability-insights-for-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Workload Analysis</a> scans your <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudtrail/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS CloudTrail</a> logs and CloudFormation stacks, then narrows 200+ services to the 20–30 your account actually runs, significantly reducing the scope of a Regional gap analysis.</p> <p>Together with the <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/building-automated-aws-regional-availability-checks-with-amazon-s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Capabilities by Region S3 Access Point</a>, you now have three levels of control: consume from Amazon S3, deploy into your VPC, or filter to your workload. We walk through how to deploy each solution, run a workload analysis, and view personalized results during your Regional expansion planning. Whether you’re building multi-Region recovery strategies, standardizing compliance reporting, or accelerating expansion timelines, these solutions put the data and the decisions it drives inside your perimeter.</p> <p>Figure 1 shows how Capability Insights for AWS integrates with the VPC and subnets in your existing AWS account. A client in the public subnet accesses the dashboard through an S3 gateway endpoint and calls the private API through an API Gateway VPC endpoint.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/30/ARCHBLOG-1522-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/30/ARCHBLOG-1522-1.png" alt="Capability Insights for AWS architecture showing a client in the public subnet reaching the dashboard through an S3 gateway endpoint and the private API through an API Gateway VPC endpoint" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 1: Capability Insights for AWS architecture, including private dashboard access and scheduled Regional availability data refreshes.</p> </div> <p>An Amazon EventBridge schedule invokes the data-fetch Lambda function every 24 hours. The data-fetch function runs outside your VPC, so it reads the S3 access point published by AWS over the AWS network. The function pulls Regional availability data from the Capabilities by Region S3 bucket published by AWS and writes it to the website bucket within your account. The dashboard application accesses the data within the website through the S3 gateway endpoint from your VPC. The API Lambda can also invoke the data-fetch function on demand through the Lambda VPC endpoint. The deployment assets bucket supplies the Lambda code during deployment only.</p> <h2 id="prerequisites">Prerequisites</h2> <p>To follow along, you need:</p> <ul> <li>An AWS account with permissions to deploy the CloudFormation stacks, including permission to create named <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)</a> roles. At runtime, the roles created by the stacks use scoped permissions for Amazon S3 object read and write access, AWS CloudFormation read operations, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Athena</a> queries, AWS Glue and AWS Lake Formation catalog access, AWS Lambda invocation, and AWS Step Functions execution. For starting-point deployment policies, see the <a href="https://github.com/aws/capability-insights-for-aws/tree/main/docs" target="_blank" rel="noopener">documentation</a> folder in the repository.</li> <li><a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)</a> installed and configured.</li> <li>A VPC with DNS resolution and DNS hostnames enabled, one public subnet (routed to an internet gateway) for dashboard and API access, and one private subnet (no internet route) for the in-VPC Lambda function. Both subnets need a route to Amazon S3 through a gateway VPC endpoint.</li> <li><a href="https://nodejs.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Node.js v24.18.0</a> (includes npm and npx) for automated deployment.</li> <li>An S3 bucket for deployment assets.</li> <li>An active CloudTrail configuration where you store logs in an S3 bucket (required for Part 2).</li> </ul> <h2 id="part-1-deploy-a-self-hosted-dashboard-capability-insights-for-aws">Part 1: Deploy a self-hosted dashboard (Capability Insights for AWS)</h2> <p>Capability Insights for AWS deploys a searchable Regional availability dashboard into your own AWS account. The solution pulls data from the AWS Capabilities by Region S3 bucket, stores it inside your VPC, and serves it through a static website backed by <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/api-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon API Gateway</a>, <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Lambda</a>, and <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eventbridge/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon EventBridge</a>.</p> <p>If your organization has access to additional data sources beyond the public dataset, the solution incorporates those as well, giving you a unified view across all AWS partitions you have access to. To learn more about accessing additional data, work with your AWS representative.</p> <p>The dashboard covers:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Services and features</strong>: availability status, expected launch dates, and expansion plans per Region.</li> <li><strong>API operations</strong>: individual API action availability per Region for each AWS service.</li> <li><strong>CloudFormation resource types</strong>: which resource types each Region supports.</li> </ul> <p>You provide your own VPC, subnets, and S3 bucket so the solution integrates with your existing infrastructure and security controls.</p> <h3 id="clone-and-install">Clone and install</h3> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">git clone https://github.com/aws/capability-insights-for-aws.git cd capability-insights-for-aws npm install</code></pre> </div> <h3 id="create-a-deployment-assets-bucket">Create a deployment assets bucket</h3> <p>Create an S3 bucket with public access blocked to store the Lambda code package during deployment. We recommend naming it <code>capability-insights-assets-&lt;ACCOUNT_ID&gt;-&lt;REGION&gt;</code>.</p> <h3 id="deploy-the-stack">Deploy the stack</h3> <p><strong>Automated deployment:</strong></p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">npm run deploy</code></pre> </div> <p>The script builds all assets, prompts for parameters, deploys the CloudFormation stack, uploads the website, and triggers an initial data sync. You will be prompted for <code>SourceFolders</code>, a comma-separated list of data sources to pull from. The default is <code>public</code>.</p> <p>To skip interactive prompts, pass all parameters as flags:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">npm run deploy -- \ --private-vpc-id vpc-0abc123 \ --backend-subnet-id subnet-0abc123 \ --api-access-subnet-id subnet-0def456 \ --deployment-assets-bucket-name my-deploy-bucket \ --source-access-point-arn arn:aws:s3:us-east-1:686591367145:accesspoint/aws-capabilities-public \ --source-folders public</code></pre> </div> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Flag</strong></td> <td><strong>Description</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td>–private-vpc-id</td> <td>VPC ID with DNS resolution and DNS hostnames enabled</td> </tr> <tr> <td>–backend-subnet-id</td> <td>Private subnet with no internet route. Needs a route to Amazon S3 through a gateway VPC endpoint.</td> </tr> <tr> <td>–api-access-subnet-id</td> <td>Public subnet routed to an internet gateway (user access, API Gateway VPC endpoint)</td> </tr> <tr> <td>–deployment-assets-bucket-name</td> <td>S3 bucket for deployment assets</td> </tr> <tr> <td>–source-access-point-arn</td> <td> <p>Public Capabilities by Region S3 access point ARN published by AWS. Account ID 686591367145 identifies the account managed by AWS that hosts the public dataset. Use the ARN as shown.</p> <p>For details on the public dataset, see <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/building-automated-aws-regional-availability-checks-with-amazon-s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Building automated AWS Regional availability checks with Amazon S3</a>.</p> </td> </tr> <tr> <td>–source-folders</td> <td>Comma-separated data sources (default: public)</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p><strong>Manual deployment:</strong></p> <p>If your organization requires deploying with native AWS tooling only, download <code>build-assets.zip</code> from the <a href="https://github.com/aws/capability-insights-for-aws/releases/latest" target="_blank" rel="noopener">latest release</a> and follow these steps:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash"># Upload Lambda code aws s3 cp lambda/lambdaAssets.zip s3://&lt;DEPLOYMENT_ASSETS_BUCKET&gt;/lambdaAssets.zip # Deploy the stack aws cloudformation deploy \ --template-file template/capability-insights.template.json \ --stack-name CapabilityInsightsForAWS \ --capabilities CAPABILITY_IAM CAPABILITY_NAMED_IAM \ --parameter-overrides \ PrivateVpcId=&lt;VPC_ID&gt; \ BackendSubnetId=&lt;BACKEND_SUBNET_ID&gt; \ ApiAccessSubnetId=&lt;API_ACCESS_SUBNET_ID&gt; \ DeploymentAssetsBucketName=&lt;DEPLOYMENT_ASSETS_BUCKET&gt; \ DeploymentAssetsBucketApiLambdaFunctionCodeZipPath=lambdaAssets.zip \ SourceAccessPointArn=arn:aws:s3:us-east-1:686591367145:accesspoint/aws-capabilities-public \ SourceFolders=public # Upload website assets aws s3 sync website/ s3://capability-insights-website-&lt;ACCOUNT_ID&gt;-&lt;REGION&gt;/ # Trigger initial data sync aws lambda invoke \ --function-name CapabilityInsightsDataFetchLambda \ --invocation-type Event /dev/null</code></pre> </div> <h3 id="access-the-dashboard">Access the dashboard</h3> <p>The solution hosts the website on S3, accessible only from within your VPC:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-plaintext">http://capability-insights-website-&lt;ACCOUNT_ID&gt;-&lt;REGION&gt;.s3-website-&lt;REGION&gt;.amazonaws.com</code></pre> </div> <p>Because the solution you deploy hosts the website without public access, you need connectivity to the VPC. Common options:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Existing VPN or AWS Direct Connect</strong>: use your organization’s existing connectivity.</li> <li><strong>AWS Client VPN</strong>: set up a <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/vpn/latest/clientvpn-admin/what-is.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Client VPN endpoint</a> in the VPC.</li> <li><strong>EC2 instance with SOCKS proxy</strong>: SSH into an instance in the VPC and proxy browser traffic through it.</li> </ul> <h3 id="explore-the-dashboard">Explore the dashboard</h3> <p>Once connected, the dashboard provides:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Search and filter</strong>: find services by name across all Regions.</li> <li><strong>Expandable service details</strong>: select any service to see individual feature availability per Region.</li> <li><strong>Status indicators</strong>: Available, Planning, Not Expanding, and projected dates (for example, “2026 Q3”)</li> <li><strong>Export</strong>: download the current view as JSON or CSV for sharing or further analysis.</li> <li><strong>Settings</strong>: view last sync time and trigger manual data refreshes.</li> </ul> <p>Figure 2 shows the Capability Insights for AWS dashboard and its controls for comparing service availability across Regions.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/30/ARCHBLOG-1522-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/30/ARCHBLOG-1522-2.png" alt="Capability Insights for AWS dashboard listing services with per-Region availability status indicators, summary counts, and search, filter, and export controls" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 2: Dashboard overview showing service availability across Regions with status indicators and export options.</p> </div> <p>Summary counts show coverage for services and features, API operations, CloudFormation resources, and Regions. Tabs switch between catalog views, while filtering, Region columns, status values, and the expand and export controls help you inspect and download availability data.</p> <p>At this point you have a working dashboard inside your VPC, refreshing daily, with no external dependencies during normal operation.</p> <p>Part 2 adds personalization by filtering this catalog to the specific services your account runs. You can view the workload analysis results through the dashboard or access them through dedicated API endpoints.</p> <h2 id="part-2-personalize-the-catalog-with-workload-analysis">Part 2: Personalize the catalog with Workload Analysis</h2> <p>The catalog covers 200+ services across 35+ Regions. When you plan expansion to a new Region, you don’t need to evaluate all of them. You need to evaluate the 20–30 your account runs. Without that filter, a gap analysis is a multi-week project. With it, it’s a 30-minute review.</p> <p>Workload Analysis deploys as an additive CloudFormation stack alongside the Capability Insights stack. The pipeline runs as an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/step-functions/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Step Functions</a> state machine with three stages:</p> <ol type="1"> <li><strong>Parallel analyzers</strong> (two branches run in parallel): <ol type="a"> <li><strong>CloudTrail Analyzer</strong>: Creates an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/glue/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS Glue</a> Data Catalog table pointing at your CloudTrail log bucket, then runs an <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Amazon Athena</a> query that extracts distinct service/API/Region/account combinations from the last N days (configurable, default 30). This identifies services your account has called.</li> <li><strong>CloudFormation Analyzer</strong>: Calls <code>ListStacks</code> and <code>GetTemplate</code> for every active stack in the account. Extracts <code>AWS::</code> resource types and scalar property values (strings, numbers, booleans), maps them to service names, and records which stack contributed each resource. This identifies what your account deploys, not only what it calls.</li> </ol></li> <li><strong>Usage Decorator</strong> runs after both analyzers complete. It reads the primary capability catalogs (<code>products.json</code>, <code>apis.json</code>, <code>cfn_resources.json</code>) from the website bucket, intersects them with the analyzer outputs, and writes personalized files back to the bucket for the dashboard to consume.</li> <li><strong>Scheduled execution</strong> through an Amazon EventBridge rule triggers the state machine daily (configurable schedule expression), keeping personalized data fresh without manual intervention.</li> </ol> <p>Figure 3 shows how the Workload Analysis components coordinate scheduled and on-demand analysis.</p> <div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"> <a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/30/ARCHBLOG-1522-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"><img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/fc074d501302eb2b93e2554793fcaf50b3bf7291/2026/09/30/ARCHBLOG-1522-3.png" alt="Workload Analysis architecture where an EventBridge schedule or API request starts a Step Functions state machine running the CloudTrail and CloudFormation analyzers in parallel, then a Usage Decorator writes personalized data to the website bucket" width="800"></a> <p class="wp-caption-text">Figure 3: Workload Analysis infrastructure components.</p> </div> <p>An Amazon EventBridge schedule or an on-demand API request starts the AWS Step Functions state machine. The workflow runs two branches in parallel: the CloudTrail analyzer uses Amazon Athena with AWS Glue Data Catalog and AWS Lake Formation catalog access to query activity, while the CloudFormation analyzer inspects active stacks. After both branches complete, the Usage Decorator combines their results with the primary capability catalogs and writes personalized data to the Capability Insights website bucket for the dashboard and API to consume.</p> <h3 id="deploy-workload-analysis">Deploy Workload Analysis</h3> <p>Verify that CloudTrail is active and your logs land in an S3 bucket. Then deploy with the usage analysis flag:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">npm run deploy -- \ --private-vpc-id vpc-0abc123 \ --backend-subnet-id subnet-0abc123 \ --api-access-subnet-id subnet-0def456 \ --deployment-assets-bucket-name my-deploy-bucket \ --source-access-point-arn arn:aws:s3:us-east-1:686591367145:accesspoint/aws-capabilities-public \ --source-folders public \ --enable-usage-analysis \ --cloudtrail-bucket my-cloudtrail-logs-bucket</code></pre> </div> <p>This deploys both the core Insights stack and the Usage Analysis stack, then wires them together so the API Lambda can trigger analysis and serve personalized results.</p> <table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"> <tbody> <tr> <td><strong>Additional flag</strong></td> <td><strong>Description</strong></td> </tr> <tr> <td><code>--enable-usage-analysis</code></td> <td>Enables the Workload Analysis pipeline</td> </tr> <tr> <td><code>--cloudtrail-bucket</code></td> <td>S3 bucket containing your CloudTrail logs</td> </tr> </tbody> </table> <p>A typical first run completes in 2–5 minutes depending on account size and the number of active CloudFormation stacks.</p> <h3 id="retrieve-api-endpoint">Retrieve API endpoint</h3> <p>Use the following command to retrieve the API base URL from the API configuration file on the dashboard bucket:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">aws s3 cp "s3://capability-insights-website-$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)-$(aws configure get region)/api-config.json" - --no-progress</code></pre> </div> <p>The command returns output similar to the following:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-json">{"apiBaseUrl": "https://xxxxxxxxxx.execute-api.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/prod"}</code></pre> </div> <p>Use <code>apiBaseUrl</code> as the base URL for the dedicated API endpoints. You must run API requests from within the VPC because the API Gateway endpoint is private.</p> <h3 id="run-an-analysis">Run an analysis</h3> <p>Start an analysis by calling <code>POST /analysis</code> through the API Gateway endpoint:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-json">{ "scope": "account", "analyzers": ["cloudtrail", "cloudformation"], "analyzerParams": { "cloudtrail": { "bucket": "my-cloudtrail-logs-bucket", "daysToScan": 30 } } }</code></pre> </div> <p>The Amazon EventBridge rule triggers subsequent runs daily. You can also trigger ad-hoc runs through the dashboard’s Settings page.</p> <h3 id="view-personalized-results">View personalized results</h3> <p>After an analysis completes, the dashboard toggles between the full catalog and your personalized <em>My Stuff</em> view, showing only services and resources your account uses.</p> <p><strong>Through the API:</strong></p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-plaintext">GET /capabilities?usageFilter=combined&amp;scope=account</code></pre> </div> <p>The response contains filtered products, APIs, and CloudFormation resources with usage attribution, including which stacks deploy each resource type and which property configurations you use:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-json">{ "products": [ { "productId": "amzn1.prod.abc123", "productName": "Amazon DynamoDB", "childProducts": [...], "regionalAvailability": { "us-east-1": "Available", "eu-west-1": "Available" } } ], "cfnResources": [ { "serviceName": "DynamoDB", "resourceTypes": [ { "resourceTypeName": "Table", "regionalAvailability": { ... }, "usage": { "stacks": ["MyAppStack", "DataPipelineStack"], "properties": { "BillingMode": ["PAY_PER_REQUEST"] }, "count": 2 } } ] } ], "lastAnalyzedAt": "2026-05-19T08:00:00.000Z" }</code></pre> </div> <h3 id="practical-example">Practical example</h3> <p>Consider an account running a typical web application. Without Workload Analysis, the dashboard shows all 200+ services across 35 Regions. With the combined filter applied:</p> <ul> <li><strong>Before</strong>: 200+ services, thousands of features, hundreds of CloudFormation resource types.</li> <li><strong>After</strong>: 28 services your account uses, with per-stack attribution showing which CloudFormation stacks deploy each resource type.</li> </ul> <p>The CloudFormation resource view goes deeper. For each resource type your stacks deploy, you can see which property configurations are in use and which stacks contributed them. For example, your <code>AWS::EC2::Instance</code> resources might show <code>InstanceType: t3.medium</code> from your application stack and <code>InstanceType: m5.xlarge</code> from your data processing stack, each traced back to its source.</p> <p>This targeted view means your Regional expansion gap analysis focuses on the 28 services you care about rather than the full catalog. For example, if you plan to deploy into the Europe (Zurich) Region (eu-central-2), the dashboard shows which 28 services are available there, which have planned launch dates, and which have no roadmap entry yet. These results highlight service-availability gaps to consider during Regional expansion. From here, you can evaluate your options: wait for planned launches, architect around unavailable features, or evaluate a different target Region. You’re not evaluating 200+ services against a Region matrix. You’re scanning a personalized list where every row is something your stacks deploy.</p> <h2 id="clean-up">Clean up</h2> <p>To remove the resources created in this walkthrough:</p> <p><strong>Workload Analysis (if deployed):</strong> Delete the Usage Analysis stack first:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">aws cloudformation delete-stack --stack-name CapabilityInsightsUsageAnalysis aws cloudformation wait stack-delete-complete --stack-name CapabilityInsightsUsageAnalysis</code></pre> </div> <p><strong>Self-hosted dashboard:</strong> Empty the website bucket (static assets, capability data, and usage analysis output), then delete the stack:</p> <div class="hide-language"> <pre><code class="language-bash">aws s3 rm s3://capability-insights-website-&lt;ACCOUNT_ID&gt;-&lt;REGION&gt; --recursive aws cloudformation delete-stack --stack-name CapabilityInsightsForAWS</code></pre> </div> <p>Optionally delete the deployment assets bucket you created during setup. This solution uses standard AWS service pricing for Lambda, S3, API Gateway, Athena, and Step Functions. There is no additional charge for the solution itself.</p> <p><strong>Automated cleanup:</strong> Run <code>npm run teardown</code> to remove both stacks and empty the website bucket in one step.</p> <h2 id="conclusion">Conclusion</h2> <p>Knowing which services are available in your target Region is the first step in designing for resilience. You can’t build a multi-Region recovery strategy for services that aren’t there yet. With Capability Insights for AWS and Workload Analysis, you own that data inside your VPC, filtered to what your account runs, refreshing on your schedule.</p> <p>Start here:</p> <ul> <li>Clone the <a href="https://github.com/aws/capability-insights-for-aws" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Capability Insights for AWS repository</a> and run <code>npm run deploy</code> to deploy a working dashboard in under 10 minutes.</li> <li>Add <code>--enable-usage-analysis</code> to personalize the catalog to your account’s real footprint.</li> <li>For CloudFormation concepts and deployment guidance, see the <a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSCloudFormation/latest/UserGuide/Welcome.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener">AWS CloudFormation User Guide</a>.</li> <li>For zero-infrastructure programmatic access to the raw data, see <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/storage/building-automated-aws-regional-availability-checks-with-amazon-s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Building automated AWS Regional availability checks with Amazon S3</a>.</li> <li>For automated expansion analysis using this data, see <a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/infrastructure-and-automation/accelerate-region-expansion-with-the-aws-knowledge-mcp-server/" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Accelerate Region expansion with the AWS Knowledge MCP server</a>.</li> </ul> <p>&nbsp;</p> <p style="clear: both"></p> <hr style="width: 100%"> <h2>About the authors</h2>
